Amazon Bedrock AgentCore

One consolidated reference for the six AgentCore services. This pane is the cross-service view — how the pieces compose, what each one costs, the limits that actually bind, and the lessons that repeat across all of them. Use the switcher above to open any individual service reference.

Document revision: 18 August 2026  ·  Platform status: GA 13 October 2025  ·  Services covered: Runtime · Memory · Gateway · Identity · Observability · Policy
How to read this

Each service reference opens with an Architect's brief — the decisions that shape a design, separated from the reference material you can look up later. If you are new to AgentCore, read this pane, then the briefs of the two or three services you actually need. That is roughly thirty minutes and covers most of what determines whether a design works.

1. What AgentCore is #

AgentCore is not one product. It is six composable services that each solve a distinct problem that shows up when an AI agent moves from a notebook to production. You can adopt any one of them alone — none requires the others — and most teams start with two.

Runtime

Serverless hosting with per-session microVM isolation, 8-hour sessions (14 days on Instances), and consumption billing that skips CPU charges during I/O wait.

Memory

Short-term event log plus asynchronously extracted long-term records, with managed consolidation and semantic retrieval scoped by namespace.

Gateway

An AI gateway that turns APIs, Lambda functions, MCP servers and model providers into governed, discoverable tools behind one endpoint.

Identity

Agent identity plus a credential vault, binding every credential to the pair of which agent and on whose behalf.

Observability

OpenTelemetry-native sessions, traces and spans in CloudWatch, plus LLM-as-judge Evaluations over the same traces.

Policy

Cedar-based authorization enforced at the Gateway boundary — deterministic rules the model cannot argue with.

1.1 The problem each one actually solves

ServiceWithout it you would…The hard part it takes off you
RuntimeRun agents on Lambda (15-min ceiling) or ECS (pay for idle, build isolation yourself)Per-session isolation as a platform property, and not paying for the 70% of wall-clock an agent spends waiting
MemoryBuild extract → embed → dedupe → merge yourselfConsolidation — deciding whether a new fact is novel, refines an existing record, or is redundant, forever, per user
GatewayWrite an MCP server per tool and hand agents credentialsThe M×N integration problem, plus credential exchange and semantic tool discovery
IdentityBuild an OAuth broker with per-user token storageThe 3LO consent dance, token caching bound to user+agent, and never giving the agent a long-lived secret
ObservabilityStare at logs and guessGenAI-aware trace structure — and Evaluations, the only layer that catches confidently-wrong answers
PolicyPut the rule in a system prompt and hopeDeterministic, analysable enforcement outside the model, where injection cannot reach it

2.1 The dependency map

If you use…You will likely also want…Because
RuntimeObservabilitySessions and metrics come free; traces need two opt-ins you should make on day one
RuntimeMemoryRuntime state dies with the session (8 h / 14 d). Anything that must outlive it goes to Memory
GatewayIdentityEvery API-key or OAuth target credential lives in the Identity token vault — and Identity is free when used through Gateway or Runtime
GatewayPolicyPolicy only enforces at the Gateway boundary. No gateway, nothing to enforce against
IdentityMemoryThe verified user identity from Identity is what should drive Memory's actorId
ObservabilityeverythingPolicy decisions, Identity token fetches, Gateway target timings and Memory operations all surface here
The one composition worth calling out

Identity → Memory. Memory files credentials and records under a user identity, and Identity is the only component that verifies that identity cryptographically. Wiring Memory's actorId from an unverified string rather than a validated JWT subject is the single most common way a multi-tenant agent leaks one user's memory to another.

3. What to adopt first #

If your situation is…Start withAdd next
Prototype agent, want it hosted and observableRuntime + ObservabilityMemory once conversations need continuity
Agent needs to remember users across sessionsMemoryIdentity, to make actorId trustworthy
Several agents need the same toolsGatewayIdentity for target credentials; Policy for guardrails
Agent must act on a user's own SaaS dataIdentity + GatewayPolicy, before it touches anything irreversible
Agent can spend money or delete thingsPolicy (in LOG_ONLY)Observability + Evaluations to prove it behaves
Agent already runs elsewhere (ECS, on-prem, another cloud)ObservabilityMemory and Identity — both work standalone
You do not need to buy the platform

Every service here is a plain AWS API. Memory works from a Lambda. Identity works from ECS. Observability ingests telemetry from on-premises or another cloud via ADOT and SigV4. The only hard coupling is Policy requires Gateway. Treat AgentCore as six tools, not a framework you adopt wholesale.

4. The lessons that repeat #

Six independent services, but the same handful of themes produce most production surprises. These are drawn from the individual Architect's briefs.

4.1 Identifiers are one-way doors

Runtime · session ID

Drives isolation and microVM affinity. Never reuse across users; always reuse within a conversation.

Memory · actorId

Defines what can ever be recalled together. Changing it orphans everything already written.

Identity · user ID namespace

The vault key. Federate a second IdP without an IdP prefix and two humans collide into one entry.

Gateway · target name

Becomes part of every tool name (Target___tool), baked into prompts, Lambda routing and Cedar policies.

Policy · action strings

Embed that target name. A rename turns working policies into non-matching ones — which means denied.

Observability · session ID

The same value again. Get it wrong and the sessions view, cross-turn debugging and session-level evaluators all break.

The pattern: every service has one identifier chosen early that cannot be changed later without abandoning data or breaking references. Spend an hour on naming before you ship anything.

4.2 Some capabilities cannot be retrofitted

SettingServiceConsequence of getting it wrong
searchType (semantic tool search)GatewayCreation-time only. Retrofit = new gateway + repoint every client
Consistent session IDsRuntime · ObservabilityNo way to re-group historical telemetry
Trace sampling percentageObservabilityUnsampled traffic is gone; raising it is not retroactive
New strategies on an existing MemoryMemoryApply forward only; no backfill of historical events
Indexed keys for metadata filtersMemoryCannot be removed, and never backfilled onto existing records
User-ID namespacingIdentityRetrofit means every user re-consents

4.3 The limits that actually bind

In every service, the quota people hit is not the headline number.

ServiceHeadline quotaWhat actually binds
Runtime1,000 TPS data planeActive sessions (5,000) — driven by idle timeout, not traffic
Memory200 TPS CreateEvent5 TPS per actor per session, not adjustable
Gateway200 TPS tool calls25 searches per minute — three orders of magnitude lower
Identity200 TPS token retrieval50 credential providers per type
Policy1,000 policies per engine400 KB Cedar schema, which grows with tool count
Observability—CloudWatch ingestion volume, which is a bill rather than a limit

4.4 Costs are shaped opposite to intuition

Memory: reading, not storing

Retrieval at $0.50/1K dominates storage at $0.75/1K-per-month. Two retrievals per turn can be 8× the storage bill. Fetch preferences once per session, not per turn.

Gateway: search, not calls

Tool invocations are $0.005/1K — negligible. Search is 5×, and Web Search is 1,400×. Search for discovery, never dispatch.

Runtime: idle memory, not CPU

No CPU charge during I/O wait, but memory accrues throughout — including idle. Leaked sessions are the classic surprise line item.

Observability: the platform is free, CloudWatch is not

Zero AgentCore charge; you pay ingestion, storage and query on dozens of payload-bearing spans per turn.

4.5 Two things are never automatic

Neither errors if absent

Traces require two opt-ins — Transaction Search enabled once per account, and ADOT instrumentation. Metrics appear either way, so teams conclude observability works and discover during their first incident that they have counters without causality.

Policy in ENFORCE is default-deny. Attaching an engine with an incomplete policy set blocks every tool it doesn't explicitly permit. Always roll out in LOG_ONLY first.

4.6 Security themes that cut across

Isolation is a platform property, not your code

Runtime's per-session microVM means a caching bug is a one-session incident rather than a cross-tenant disclosure. Don't reintroduce sharing by accepting client-supplied session IDs.

Agents should never hold long-lived secrets

Identity's vault issues short-lived, user-scoped tokens. Gateway injects target credentials the agent never sees. Environment-variable secrets defeat both.

Persistence turns injection into a lasting problem

A poisoned "fact" in Memory is extracted, consolidated and retrieved into every future conversation. Validate on the way in, not just out.

Enforcement belongs outside the model

Policy at the Gateway boundary is deterministic and unreachable by prompts. A system prompt is a suggestion; Cedar is a rule.

Telemetry is a data-exfiltration surface

request_payload and response_payload are logged by default. That is what makes debugging work — and how PII reaches CloudWatch.

Lock the back doors

Enforce gateway-only inbound traffic on backends, and restrict UpdateGateway — it is the only control on whether Policy is enforced at all.

4.7 Smaller and scoped beats big and general

Four independent findings converge on the same architecture. Gateway: tool overload degrades model accuracy long before it hits a quota. Policy: the 400 KB Cedar schema grows with tool count, and AWS's own remedy is to split engines per gateway. Identity: one workload identity per trust tier is what makes scope-down policies meaningful. Memory: over-nested namespaces destroy retrieval precision.

The synthesis: prefer several purpose-scoped gateways, engines and identities over one large shared everything. A support agent should not see the deployment tools, and its policy engine should not have to carry their schema.

5.1 A rough sense of proportion

text
Ten million agent tool calls in a month, with memory and policy enabled:

  Gateway invocations   10 M / 1,000 × $0.005          = $    50
  Policy authorizations 10 M      × $0.000025          = $   250
  Memory retrievals      4 M / 1,000 × $0.50           = $ 2,000
  Runtime compute        (per §Runtime worked example) ≈ $    76 / 100k turns
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  … and model tokens, which typically dominate all of the above.

  Add ONE Web Search per task at 1 M tasks              = $ 7,000   ← 20× everything else

Two takeaways. Model inference is usually the bill; AgentCore's own charges are often a rounding error beside it. And the exceptions are Memory retrieval and Web Search — both easy to put in a per-turn loop without noticing, and both worth an explicit design decision.

HTTP passthrough targets, inference targets, Runtime targets and interceptors turn Gateway into a three-plane AI gateway. Memory adds structured metadata filtering. Identity adds on-behalf-of exchange and VPC egress. Traces drop below 10-second latency.

Cedar authorization across thirteen regions. Evaluations with built-in evaluators and ground truth. Runtime gains managed session storage, shell execution and the AG-UI protocol. Memory adds resource-based policies and record streaming.

8. Production checklist #

A condensed pass across all six. Each item links to the service pane that explains it.

AreaCheck
NamingSession IDs, actorId, user-ID namespacing, gateway target names and provider names all decided and frozen
RuntimeARM64 images · numeric USER · idleRuntimeSessionTimeout tuned · StopRuntimeSession on conversation end · async tasks completed in finally
MemoryNamespaces end with / · retrieval tuned per namespace · turns batched into single events · deletion runbook covers events and records
GatewaySemantic search enabled at creation · Lambda handlers strip the ___ prefix · backends restricted to gateway-only inbound traffic
IdentityGetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId explicitly denied · callback URLs registered · 3LO callback validates the live session
PolicyRolled out in LOG_ONLY first · every context read guarded with has · UpdateGateway restricted and alarmed
ObservabilityTransaction Search enabled and spans confirmed arriving · sampling chosen deliberately · log retention set · payload redaction decided
AlarmsActiveSessionCount at ~70% of quota · throttles · NoDeterminingPolicies · Identity token-fetch failures · Evaluation score trends
CostMemory retrieval per session not per turn · Web Search rate-limited · tags activated for allocation
If you only do three things

1. Get session IDs and actorId right before shipping — they are unrecoverable later. 2. Enable Transaction Search and confirm spans arrive, before you need them. 3. Put anything irreversible behind a Policy engine, rolled out in LOG_ONLY.

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime

A complete technical reference for the serverless, session-isolated hosting service for AI agents and agent tools on AWS — architecture, service contracts, deployment, APIs, identity, networking, observability, quotas, pricing, and troubleshooting.

Document revision: 18 August 2026  ·  Service status: Generally available (GA 13 Oct 2025)  ·  Control plane: bedrock-agentcore-control  ·  Data plane: bedrock-agentcore
Fast-moving service

AgentCore ships frequently — Instances (Aug 2026), unified trace destinations (Jul 2026), quota increases (Jun/Jul 2026), S3/EFS mounts (May 2026), AG-UI + shells + session storage (Mar 2026), Node.js code deploy + VPC egress (Apr 2026). Every figure below reflects AWS documentation as of the revision date. Re-check quotas and pricing against the live AWS docs before designing to a hard limit.

1. Overview #

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime is a purpose-built, serverless hosting environment for AI agents and agent tools. You bring agent code written in any framework, calling any model, and AgentCore Runtime turns it into a scalable, secure, identity-aware cloud endpoint — without you managing servers, containers orchestration, autoscaling, or session isolation.

The core design premise is that agents are not ordinary request/response web services. They run for minutes or hours rather than milliseconds. They spend most wall-clock time waiting on model tokens and tool I/O rather than burning CPU. They accumulate mutable state — scratch files, installed packages, reasoning context — that must never leak between users. And they behave non-deterministically, so security cannot depend on the agent "behaving well." Runtime is built around those four facts.

8 h
Max microVM session
14 d
Max Instances session
100 MB
Max payload
1 : 1
Session : microVM
4
Wire protocols

Max hardware per microVM session

1.1 Defining characteristics

Session isolation by microVM

Every session gets a dedicated microVM with its own CPU, memory and filesystem. On termination the microVM is destroyed and memory sanitised. Isolation is a property of the platform, not of your code.

Framework and model agnostic

Strands, LangGraph/LangChain, CrewAI, Google ADK, OpenAI Agents, or hand-written code. Bedrock models, Anthropic, OpenAI, Gemini, or a self-hosted endpoint. Runtime only cares about the HTTP contract.

Long-horizon execution

Up to 8 hours per microVM session, 14 days on Instances — versus the 15-minute ceiling of AWS Lambda. Background/async work is first-class via health-status signalling.

Consumption-based billing

Billed per second on actual vCPU consumed and peak memory. CPU accrues no charge while the agent is blocked waiting on an LLM or an API — often the majority of a session.

Four wire protocols

HTTP (REST + SSE + WebSocket), MCP (tool servers), A2A (agent-to-agent), and AG-UI (interactive UI event streams) — selected per runtime.

Built-in identity & tracing

Inbound SigV4 or OIDC/JWT authorization, workload identity for outbound OAuth, and OpenTelemetry-based traces into CloudWatch GenAI Observability.

2. Where Runtime fits in AgentCore #

AgentCore is a family of composable services. Runtime is the hosting layer; the others are optional and independently usable — you can host on Runtime without using any of them, or use Memory/Gateway from an agent hosted elsewhere.

ServiceResponsibilityRelationship to Runtime
RuntimeHosts and executes agents/tools; sessions, scaling, isolation—
MemoryShort- and long-term memory stores with extraction/consolidation strategiesState that must outlive a session (Runtime state is per-session)
GatewayTurns APIs, Lambda functions and MCP servers into governed agent toolsCalled by agents running on Runtime; itself often an MCP endpoint
IdentityWorkload identities, token vault, inbound/outbound OAuthDirectly embedded — Runtime issues each agent a workload identity
ObservabilityGenAI-aware traces, spans, metrics, session views in CloudWatchRuntime emits spans/logs/metrics automatically when instrumented
Browser Tool / Code InterpreterManaged sandboxed browser and code-execution toolsCalled as tools from an agent on Runtime
EvaluationsTrajectory and outcome evaluation of agent sessions (GA Mar 2026)Consumes Runtime traces; drivable from the AgentCore CLI
Not the same as "Bedrock Agents"

Amazon Bedrock Agents is a managed, declarative agent (you configure action groups, knowledge bases and an orchestration prompt; AWS runs the loop). AgentCore Runtime is the opposite trade: you own the agent loop, framework and prompts entirely, and AWS runs the infrastructure underneath it. Choose Runtime when you need control over orchestration logic or want to bring an existing OSS-framework agent.

3. When to use AgentCore Runtime #

DimensionAgentCore RuntimeAWS LambdaECS / EKS / App RunnerBedrock Agents
Max execution8 h (microVM) / 14 d (Instances)15 minUnboundedPer-invocation, managed
Per-user isolationGuaranteed — 1 microVM per sessionContainer reuse across invocationsYou build itManaged
Idle costNo CPU charge during I/O wait; memory onlyNone (but no state)Pay for running tasksPer-token
Statefulness across callsYes, sticky session + optional persistent FSNo (best-effort warm reuse)Yes, self-managedSession state managed
Payload size100 MB6 MB sync / 256 KB asyncYour choiceLimited
Agent framework freedomAnyAnyAnyNone (declarative)
Ops burdenVery lowVery lowHighLowest
Streaming to clientSSE + WebSocket, bidirectionalResponse streaming (limited)Yes, self-builtYes

3.1 Good fits

  • Multi-tenant/customer-facing agents where cross-session data leakage is unacceptable.
  • Long-running research, migration, document-processing or coding agents that exceed Lambda's 15 minutes.
  • Agents that need a scratch filesystem, shell access, or to pip install at runtime.
  • Hosting an MCP tool server with managed auth and scaling (Runtime is a first-class MCP host).
  • Interactive experiences needing token-level streaming or bidirectional WebSocket.
  • Bursty traffic where paying for idle ECS capacity is wasteful, but Lambda's model doesn't fit.

3.2 Poor fits

  • Sub-100 ms, ultra-high-QPS stateless inference wrappers — Lambda or a container service will be cheaper and simpler.
  • Workloads needing more than 2 vCPU / 8 GB in a single microVM session and not suitable for Instances.
  • Purely deterministic ETL with no agentic/LLM component.
  • Anything requiring x86-only binaries under the microVM compute type (microVM containers must be ARM64; Instances support both ARM64 and x86_64).

4. Architecture & execution model #

4.1 Request path

          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 client   │  Your app / backend / another agent                          │
          └───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │  InvokeAgentRuntime  (SigV4  |  OAuth Bearer)

                          ▼
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  AgentCore Runtime data plane (bedrock-agentcore)            │
          │  • authN/authZ (SigV4 or JWT authorizer)                     │
          │  • quota + throttle enforcement                              │
          │  • session router  ── sticky by session id ──┐               │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┘
                                                         ▼
   ┌──────────────── endpoint = version pin (DEFAULT | custom) ──────────────┐
   │                                                                        │
   │   ┌──── session A ────┐   ┌──── session B ────┐   ┌──── session C ───┐  │
   │   │  microVM          │   │  microVM          │   │  microVM         │  │
   │   │  ┌─────────────┐  │   │  ┌─────────────┐  │   │  ┌────────────┐  │  │
   │   │  │ your image  │  │   │  │ your image  │  │   │  │ your image │  │  │
   │   │  │ :8080       │  │   │  │ :8080       │  │   │  │ :8080      │  │  │
   │   │  │ /invocations│  │   │  │ /invocations│  │   │  │ /invocations│ │  │
   │   │  │ /ping  /ws  │  │   │  │ /ping  /ws  │  │   │  │ /ping /ws  │  │  │
   │   │  └─────────────┘  │   │  └─────────────┘  │   │  └────────────┘  │  │
   │   │  own CPU/mem/FS   │   │  own CPU/mem/FS   │   │  own CPU/mem/FS  │  │
   │   └───────────────────┘   └───────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘  │

              │                       │                        │
              ▼                       ▼                        ▼
     Bedrock / 3rd-party LLM    Gateway · MCP tools    CloudWatch logs · X-Ray spans

4.2 The isolation model

Each session — identified by the session ID you supply — is bound to a dedicated microVM. Nothing is shared with any other session: not CPU, not memory pages, not the filesystem, not in-process caches. When the session ends, the microVM is torn down and its memory sanitised.

This matters because agent frameworks routinely cache aggressively (embeddings, tool results, retrieved documents) in process memory. In a shared-container model, a caching bug becomes a cross-tenant data-disclosure incident. Under Runtime's model, the blast radius of such a bug is a single session — a deterministic security boundary around a non-deterministic process.

Your responsibility

AgentCore does not enforce a session-to-user mapping. Your backend must generate, store and authorise the user ↔ runtimeSessionId relationship. If two users can supply the same session ID, they share a microVM and its state. Never accept a session ID directly from an untrusted client.

4.3 Session states

StateMeaningBillingExit conditions
ActiveProcessing a request, running a command, or executing background workvCPU-seconds + memoryWork completes → Idle
IdleProvisioned and warm, awaiting the next invocation on this session IDMemory only (no CPU)New request → Active; idle timeout / max lifetime → Stopped
StoppedCompute terminated. The session identity remains validNone (persistent storage, if configured, is billed)New invocation on the same ID → fresh compute provisioned

4.4 Lifecycle in order

  1. Invoke. Client calls InvokeAgentRuntime with an ARN, a qualifier (endpoint) and a session ID.
  2. Route or provision. If a warm microVM exists for that session ID, the request goes there (session stickiness). Otherwise a new microVM is booted from your container image or code package — this is the cold start.
  3. Health gate. Runtime polls GET /ping. The container must answer 200 with {"status":"Healthy"} before traffic is delivered.
  4. Serve. The request is delivered to POST /invocations (or /mcp, /, or the WebSocket upgrade, depending on protocol). Response is JSON, SSE, or a WebSocket stream.
  5. Idle. The microVM stays warm. Subsequent invocations with the same session ID skip the cold start and see the same in-process state and filesystem.
  6. Stop. Triggered by the idle timeout (default 15 min), max lifetime (default 8 h), an explicit StopRuntimeSession, or health-check failure. Memory sanitised; unpersisted filesystem data is lost.
  7. Resume (optional). Invoking the same session ID afterwards provisions fresh compute; if session storage is configured, the filesystem is re-attached intact.

4.5 Cold starts

Cold start cost is dominated by image/package pull plus your process's import and initialisation time. Practical mitigations:

  • Keep the container image small — the 2 GB image cap is a ceiling, not a target. Direct code deployment (250 MB zipped) typically starts faster still.
  • Always reuse the session ID for a conversation. Losing stickiness means a cold start on every turn.
  • Do heavy initialisation (model client construction, tool schema loading) at module import, not per invocation — it is amortised across the whole session.
  • Raise idleRuntimeSessionTimeout for interactive workloads where a user may pause for several minutes between turns; lower it for one-shot jobs to release sessions against the concurrency quota.

5. Compute types: microVMs vs Instances #

Runtime offers two compute types behind an identical API surface. Pick per runtime; both can coexist in one architecture (for example, a lightweight coordinator on microVMs dispatching to GPU workers on Instances).

microVMs (serverless, default)Instances (EC2-backed, GA Aug 2026)
ProvisioningFully managed, on demand, per sessionAWS-managed EC2 in your account via a capacity provider
Max session lifetime8 hours (configurable up to the service max)14 days
Hardware per sessionUp to 2 vCPU / 8 GB, dynamically allocatedWhatever instance types the capacity provider allows
GPUNoYes — g4dn, g5, g6, g6e, gr6, g6f, gr6f, g7e, plus inf2. Drivers provisioned automatically
ArchitectureARM64 containers onlyLinux ARM64 and x86_64
Agents per session1 : 1 — one agent per microVM1 : N — multiple runtimes can share one instance/session (up to 20 agents per capacity-provider session)
Shared filesystemPer-session onlyAgents co-located on a session share /tmp/agentcore-session/<session-id>/
PersistenceOptional managed session storage (1 GB)EBS volumes defined on the capacity provider; survive stop/restart
Billing$/vCPU-hour + $/GB-hour, per second, no CPU charge on I/O waitEC2 instance price + AgentCore management fee (12% standard, 7.8% GPU); eligible for Savings Plans / RIs / ODCRs
Best forBursty, API-driven, short-to-medium agent turnsDays-long jobs, GPU inference, OS-level control, multi-agent collaboration on one host

5.1 Capacity providers (Instances only)

A capacity provider is a reusable infrastructure template: allowed EC2 instance types, OS, VPC and subnets, EBS volumes, and the IAM roles used for provisioning. It is created in CREATING and becomes ACTIVE after validation; afterwards only its description is editable. All associated runtimes must be disassociated before it can be deleted — deleting it stops and deletes every associated session and its persistent storage.

json
{
  "capacityProviderConfiguration": {
    "instanceTypes": ["g5.xlarge", "g5.2xlarge"],
    "vpcConfiguration": {
      "subnetIds": ["subnet-0123456789abcdef0"],
      "securityGroupIds": ["sg-0123456789abcdef0"]
    },
    "ebsVolumes": [ { "volumeSize": 100, "volumeType": "gp3" } ],
    "iamInstanceProfile": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:instance-profile/AgentCoreInstanceProfile",
    "infrastructureRole": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/AgentCoreInfraRole"
  }
}

Three distinct roles are involved and are easy to confuse:

RoleAssumed byGrants
Infrastructure roleAgentCore servicePermission to provision and manage the EC2 instances
Instance profileThe EC2 instanceSystem-level needs (e.g. log shipping). Not your agent's permissions
Runtime execution roleYour agent processBedrock, S3, Secrets Manager, and everything your agent code calls
Managed instances

Instances launched this way are Amazon EC2 managed instances: identifiable via the Operator field in DescribeInstances, hidden from the EC2 console by default, and not subject to direct EC2 lifecycle operations — but fully billable in your account.

6. The service contract #

AgentCore Runtime is protocol-driven, not SDK-driven. Anything that satisfies the HTTP contract on the right port can be hosted — the Python SDK is a convenience wrapper, not a requirement. You pick one protocol per runtime via protocolConfiguration.serverProtocol.

ProtocolserverProtocolPortPathMessage formatTypical use
HTTPHTTP8080/invocations, /wsJSON, SSE, WebSocket text/binaryConversational agents, REST-style APIs
MCPMCP8000/mcpJSON-RPC 2.0 over streamable HTTPHosting tool servers
A2AA2A9000/JSON-RPC 2.0Agent-to-agent calls, Agent Cards
AG-UIAGUI8080/invocations (SSE), /wsUI event streamsRich interactive front-ends

All four support both SigV4 and OAuth 2.0 inbound authentication. WebSocket connections can carry SigV4 via headers or query parameters.

6.1 HTTP contract

Bind to 0.0.0.0:8080. Container images for the microVM compute type must be linux/arm64.

POST /invocations — required

http
POST /invocations HTTP/1.1
Content-Type: application/json
X-Amzn-Bedrock-AgentCore-Runtime-Session-Id: 8f2c1e40-user123-conversation-0001

{ "prompt": "What's the weather today?" }

Non-streaming response:

http
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: application/json

{ "response": "Your agent's response here", "status": "success" }

Streaming response (SSE):

http
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: text/event-stream

data: {"event": "partial response 1"}

data: {"event": "partial response 2"}

data: {"event": "final response"}

GET /ping — required

The health and liveness signal. Also the mechanism by which your agent tells Runtime "I'm still working, don't reap me."

json
{ "status": "Healthy", "time_of_last_update": 1755500000 }
StatusMeaningEffect on idle timeout
HealthyIdle and ready for new workIdle timer runs; session reaped after the idle timeout
HealthyBusyOperational but executing async/background workSession kept alive beyond the idle timeout
The single most common production bug

Do not refresh time_of_last_update on every ping. Doing so makes the session look perpetually active, so it is never reaped — sessions accumulate until you hit ServiceQuotaExceededException / maxVms errors. Emit the field only when the status actually changes, or omit it entirely and let the SDK handle it.

Equally: never block inside @app.entrypoint. A blocked event loop starves the /ping handler, Runtime concludes the container is unhealthy, and your long job is killed at ~15 minutes. Push blocking work to a thread or use async I/O.

GET /ws — optional (WebSocket)

http
GET /ws HTTP/1.1
Host: agent-endpoint
Connection: Upgrade
Upgrade: websocket
Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13
Sec-WebSocket-Key: dGhlIHNhbXBsZSBub25jZQ==
X-Amzn-Bedrock-AgentCore-Runtime-Session-Id: session-uuid

Recommended message envelope:

json
{ "prompt": "Hello, can you help me?", "session_id": "session-uuid", "message_type": "user_message" }

WebSocket frames are capped at 64 KB and 250 frames/second per connection. Chunk larger payloads at the application layer.

6.2 MCP contract

Bind to 0.0.0.0:8000, serve POST /mcp, transport must be streamable-http. Responses may be application/json or text/event-stream. Clients must send Accept: application/json, text/event-stream — omitting it yields HTTP 406 with JSON-RPC error -32011.

ModeSettingBehaviour
Stateless (recommended)stateless_http=TrueThe platform generates and injects Mcp-Session-Id; your server must accept it rather than reject it, and keep state in an external store keyed by an explicit state handle returned in tool results. Plays well with AWS session routing and load balancing.
Statefulstateless_http=FalseClient initialises without a session ID, the platform returns one, and the client echoes it thereafter. Unlocks MCP elicitation (multi-turn prompts) and sampling (server-requested LLM calls). MCP spec ≥ 2026-07-28 can achieve the same with multi round-trip requests without stateful mode.

MCP-level errors are returned as HTTP 200 with a JSON-RPC error object (auth and protocol errors are the exception):

json
{ "jsonrpc": "2.0", "id": "req-001",
  "error": { "code": -32005, "message": "Session operation in progress, please retry" } }

6.3 Session-affinity headers

ProtocolHeader carrying session identity
HTTP / A2A / AG-UIX-Amzn-Bedrock-AgentCore-Runtime-Session-Id
MCPMcp-Session-Id

Capture the value returned on the first response and send it on every subsequent request. Without it, each call may land on a fresh microVM — a cold start and a loss of in-session state every turn.

6.4 Error responses

HTTPx-amzn-ErrorTypeMeaning & action
400ValidationExceptionMalformed request — fix the payload/params.
401UnauthorizedExceptionMissing/invalid bearer token on an OAuth runtime. Response carries WWW-Authenticate with the protected-resource metadata URL.
402ServiceQuotaExceededExceptionAn account quota is exhausted — usually active sessions. Reap sessions or request an increase.
403AccessDeniedExceptionIAM denial (SigV4 runtimes return 403 with no WWW-Authenticate), or container/exec-role startup failure.
404ResourceNotFoundExceptionWrong ARN, qualifier or region.
409ConflictException / RetryableConflictExceptionA session operation is in flight. Retry with short exponential backoff — AWS SDKs do this automatically for HTTP APIs; WebSocket APIs need manual retry.
424RuntimeClientErrorYour container returned 4xx/5xx or failed to mount. Go straight to CloudWatch logs.
429ThrottlingExceptionTPS limit hit — back off, or request a quota increase.
500InternalServerExceptionService-side error. Retry.
504Gateway TimeoutPort 8080 not exposed, /invocations missing, wrong CPU architecture, or a very slow cold start.

7. The Bedrock AgentCore Python SDK #

The bedrock-agentcore package implements the HTTP contract so you don't have to. It is a thin server wrapper — roughly four lines of integration around any existing agent.

bash
pip install bedrock-agentcore strands-agents
# or, preferred:
uv add bedrock-agentcore strands-agents
python
# main.py — minimal Strands agent hosted on AgentCore Runtime
from bedrock_agentcore.runtime import BedrockAgentCoreApp
from strands import Agent

app = BedrockAgentCoreApp()                 # 1. serves /invocations, /ping, /ws
agent = Agent()                             # 2. your agent — built once, at import

@app.entrypoint
def invoke(payload):                        # 3. one function per request
    user_message = payload.get("prompt", "")
    result = agent(user_message)
    return {"result": result.message}

if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run()                               # 4. binds 0.0.0.0:8080

7.1 Streaming responses

Yield from the entrypoint and the SDK emits SSE automatically:

python
@app.entrypoint
async def invoke(payload):
    stream = agent.stream_async(payload.get("prompt", ""))
    async for event in stream:
        if "data" in event:
            yield event["data"]             # → data: {...} SSE frames

7.2 Health status control

python
from bedrock_agentcore.runtime import PingStatus

@app.ping
def custom_status():
    return PingStatus.HEALTHY_BUSY if system_busy() else PingStatus.HEALTHY

7.3 What the SDK gives you

  • /invocations, /ping and WebSocket endpoints wired to the contract, including correct SSE framing.
  • Async task registration (add_async_task / complete_async_task) that flips /ping to HealthyBusy automatically — and, critically, handles time_of_last_update correctly.
  • Access to session and request context (session ID, workload access token) inside the handler.
  • Helpers for AgentCore Memory, Gateway and Identity from within a hosted agent.

SDKs also exist for TypeScript/Node and Java (Spring AI SDK for AgentCore, GA). Nothing prevents raw FastAPI/Flask/Express implementations of the contract.

8. Quickstart: Strands agent via the AgentCore CLI #

Tooling change

The Python bedrock-agentcore-starter-toolkit is now legacy. New projects should use the Node-distributed AgentCore CLI (@aws/agentcore). Both expose the agentcore command, so uninstall the old one first (pip/pipx/uv uninstall bedrock-agentcore-starter-toolkit). The runtime Python SDK (bedrock-agentcore) is unaffected and still current.

8.1 Prerequisites

  • An AWS account with credentials configured, Node.js 20+, Python 3.10+ (3.13 recommended), and uv.
  • Model access enabled in the Bedrock console for the model your agent calls.
  • boto3 ≥ 1.39.8 / botocore ≥ 1.33.8 if you plan to call the APIs directly.
  • IAM permissions to create AgentCore runtimes, IAM roles, ECR repositories and CloudFormation stacks.

8.2 Install and scaffold

bash
npm install -g @aws/agentcore
agentcore --help

# Interactive wizard
agentcore create

# Or fully non-interactive
agentcore create --name MyAgent \
  --framework Strands \
  --protocol HTTP \
  --model-provider Bedrock \
  --memory none

# Or accept every default
agentcore create --name MyAgent --defaults

Generated project layout:

text
MyAgent/
├── agentcore/
│   ├── agentcore.json      # project configuration (agents, protocol, model, resources)
│   ├── aws-targets.json    # deployment targets / account + region bindings
│   └── .env.local          # local-only environment variables
├── app/
│   └── MyAgent/
│       ├── main.py         # agent entrypoint (@app.entrypoint)
│       └── pyproject.toml  # dependencies
└── README.md

8.3 Run and test locally

bash
cd MyAgent
agentcore dev                    # dev server on http://localhost:8080 + agent inspector
agentcore dev --no-browser       # headless

# in another shell
agentcore dev "Hello, tell me a joke"

# or hit the contract directly
curl -X POST http://localhost:8080/invocations \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"prompt": "Hello!"}'

curl http://localhost:8080/ping
Test parity

Use the exact same payload shape locally and in the cloud. A large share of "works locally, 424 in AgentCore" reports are payload-shape mismatches, not infrastructure problems.

8.4 Deploy

bash
agentcore deploy                 # provisions IAM, ECR/S3, the runtime, endpoints
agentcore deploy --dry-run       # preview the change set
agentcore deploy -y              # non-interactive (CI)

agentcore status                 # ARNs, endpoints, versions, deployed resources

8.5 Invoke

bash
agentcore invoke "Tell me a joke"
agentcore invoke --prompt "Tell me a joke" --stream
agentcore invoke --session-id my-session-0001 "What else can you tell me?"
python
import json, uuid, boto3

agent_arn = "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:runtime/MyAgent-a1b2c3d4e5"
client = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore", region_name="us-west-2")

response = client.invoke_agent_runtime(
    agentRuntimeArn=agent_arn,
    runtimeSessionId=str(uuid.uuid4()),          # ≥ 33 characters
    payload=json.dumps({"prompt": "Tell me a joke"}).encode(),
    qualifier="DEFAULT",
)

chunks = [c.decode("utf-8") for c in response.get("response", [])]
print(json.loads("".join(chunks)))

8.6 Observe and clean up

bash
agentcore logs --follow           # stream runtime logs
agentcore traces list             # recent traces
agentcore traces get <trace-id>   # download one trace
agentcore run eval                # on-demand evaluation
agentcore run batch-evaluation    # evaluate across many sessions

agentcore remove all && agentcore deploy   # tear the stack back down

Console locations after deployment: logs at /aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/{agent-id}-DEFAULT in CloudWatch, the stack under CloudFormation, and the execution role under IAM (search BedrockAgentCore).

9. Packaging & deployment options #

Container deploymentDirect code deployment
ArtifactARM64 OCI image in Amazon ECRZIP in S3 (codeConfiguration)
Size limit2 GB image250 MB zipped / 750 MB unzipped
Managed runtimesn/a — you control the imagePYTHON_3_10…3_14, NODE_22
System packagesAnything you can install in the imageOnly what ships in the managed runtime + your wheels
Cold startSlower (image pull)Faster
New-session rate400 sessions/min per endpoint25 sessions/sec per endpoint
Storage costECR storageS3 Standard
Best forCustom system deps, browsers, compiled binariesFast iteration, pure-Python/Node agents

9.1 Container deployment

Requirements: linux/arm64, listening on 0.0.0.0:8080 (or 8000/9000 for MCP/A2A), implementing the contract endpoints.

dockerfile
FROM --platform=linux/arm64 public.ecr.aws/docker/library/python:3.13-slim

WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

COPY . .

# Numeric UID avoids the >53-layer overlay-mount bug that surfaces as HTTP 424
USER 1000

EXPOSE 8080
# opentelemetry-instrument enables AgentCore Observability tracing
CMD ["opentelemetry-instrument", "python", "main.py"]
bash
# Build for ARM64 even from an x86 laptop
docker buildx build --platform linux/arm64 -t my-agent:latest .

aws ecr get-login-password --region us-west-2 \
  | docker login --username AWS --password-stdin 123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-west-2.amazonaws.com
docker tag my-agent:latest 123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/my-agent:latest
docker push 123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/my-agent:latest

# Verify the contract locally before deploying
docker run -p 8080:8080 my-agent:latest
curl -X POST http://localhost:8080/invocations -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"prompt":"hi"}'

9.2 Direct code deployment

No Docker required. Package ARM64 wheels plus your source into a ZIP, upload to S3, and reference it with codeConfiguration.

bash
uv init agentcore_direct_deploy --python 3.13
cd agentcore_direct_deploy
uv add bedrock-agentcore strands-agents

# Resolve aarch64 wheels into a staging directory
uv pip install \
  --python-platform aarch64-manylinux2014 \
  --python-version 3.13 \
  --target=deployment_package \
  --only-binary=:all: \
  -r pyproject.toml

cd deployment_package && zip -r ../deployment_package.zip . && cd ..
zip deployment_package.zip main.py
python
import boto3

account_id, region = "123456789012", "us-west-2"
agent_name = "strands_direct_deploy"
bucket = f"bedrock-agentcore-code-{account_id}-{region}"

boto3.client("s3", region_name=region).upload_file(
    "deployment_package.zip", bucket, f"{agent_name}/deployment_package.zip",
    ExtraArgs={"ExpectedBucketOwner": account_id},
)

ctl = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore-control", region_name=region)
resp = ctl.create_agent_runtime(
    agentRuntimeName=agent_name,
    agentRuntimeArtifact={
        "codeConfiguration": {
            "code": {"s3": {"bucket": bucket, "prefix": f"{agent_name}/deployment_package.zip"}},
            "runtime": "PYTHON_3_13",
            "entryPoint": ["opentelemetry-instrument", "main.py"],
        }
    },
    networkConfiguration={"networkMode": "PUBLIC"},
    roleArn=f"arn:aws:iam::{account_id}:role/AmazonBedrockAgentCoreSDKRuntime-{region}",
    lifecycleConfiguration={"idleRuntimeSessionTimeout": 300, "maxLifetime": 1800},
)
print(resp["agentRuntimeArn"])
Packaging rules

Wheels must target aarch64. File permissions must be 644 for files and 755 for directories. The entrypoint must either use @app.entrypoint from the SDK or implement POST /invocations and GET /ping yourself.

9.3 Infrastructure as code

CloudFormation supports AWS::BedrockAgentCore::Runtime (plus endpoint resources), so runtimes can be managed via CDK, Terraform's AWS provider, or raw templates. The AgentCore CLI itself provisions through CloudFormation, which is why agentcore status can point you at a stack. For CI/CD, agentcore deploy -y in a GitHub Actions/CodeBuild job on an ARM64 runner is the common pattern.

10. Control plane API #

Service: bedrock-agentcore-control. Primary operations: CreateAgentRuntime, UpdateAgentRuntime, GetAgentRuntime, ListAgentRuntimes, DeleteAgentRuntime, ListAgentRuntimeVersions, plus the *AgentRuntimeEndpoint family and capacity-provider operations.

10.1 CreateAgentRuntime parameters

ParameterReq.Notes
agentRuntimeName✔Pattern [a-zA-Z][a-zA-Z0-9_]{0,47} — letters, digits and underscores only; no hyphens.
agentRuntimeArtifact✔Tagged union: containerConfiguration.containerUri or codeConfiguration.
roleArn✔Execution role assumed by the agent process.
networkConfiguration—networkMode: PUBLIC | VPC, plus subnets/security groups and requireServiceS3Endpoint.
protocolConfiguration—serverProtocol: HTTP | MCP | A2A | AGUI.
authorizerConfiguration—Omit for SigV4; supply customJWTAuthorizer for OIDC/OAuth.
lifecycleConfiguration—idleRuntimeSessionTimeout and maxLifetime, each 60–1,209,600 seconds.
environmentVariables—Plain key/value map injected into the container.
filesystemConfigurations—Session storage, S3 Files access point, EFS access point, or capacity-provider volume — each with a mountPath.
capacityProviderConfiguration—Binds the runtime to a capacity provider (Instances compute type).
requestHeaderConfiguration—requestHeaderAllowlist — custom headers passed through to your container.
metadataConfiguration—requireMMDSV2 — mandatory for all invocations from 30 Jun 2026.
tags—Cost allocation and attribute-based access control.

10.2 Full CLI example

bash
aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-agent-runtime \
  --agent-runtime-name my_runtime \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/AgentRuntimeRole \
  --agent-runtime-artifact '{
      "containerConfiguration": {
        "containerUri": "123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/my-agent:latest"
      }}' \
  --network-configuration '{
      "networkMode": "VPC",
      "networkModeConfig": {
        "securityGroups": ["sg-0123456789abcdef0"],
        "subnets": ["subnet-0123456789abcdef0", "subnet-abcdef0123456789"],
        "requireServiceS3Endpoint": true
      }}' \
  --protocol-configuration '{"serverProtocol": "HTTP"}' \
  --lifecycle-configuration '{"idleRuntimeSessionTimeout": 900, "maxLifetime": 28800}' \
  --environment-variables '{"LOG_LEVEL": "INFO"}' \
  --filesystem-configurations '[{"sessionStorage": {"mountPath": "/mnt/sessions"}}]' \
  --request-header-configuration '{"requestHeaderAllowlist": ["X-Tenant-Id"]}' \
  --tags '{"Environment": "production", "CostCenter": "engineering"}' \
  --region us-east-1

Response:

json
{
  "agentRuntimeArn": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:runtime/my_runtime-1a2b3c4d5e",
  "agentRuntimeId": "my_runtime-1a2b3c4d5e",
  "agentRuntimeVersion": "1",
  "workloadIdentityDetails": {
    "workloadIdentityArn": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default/workload-identity/my_runtime-1a2b3c4d5e"
  },
  "createdAt": "2026-08-18T10:30:45Z",
  "status": "CREATING"
}

11. Versions & endpoints #

Runtime separates what is deployed (versions) from what traffic reaches (endpoints) — the same shape as Lambda versions and aliases.

  • V1 is created automatically by CreateAgentRuntime.
  • Every UpdateAgentRuntime mints a new, immutable, fully self-contained version.
  • The DEFAULT endpoint always tracks the latest version, automatically.
  • Custom endpoints pin a specific version and only move when you explicitly update them.
  • Callers select an endpoint with the qualifier parameter on InvokeAgentRuntime.
  • Up to 1,000 versions and 10 endpoints per agent (both adjustable).
  UpdateAgentRuntime ──► V1 ──► V2 ──► V3   (immutable, append-only)
                                       ▲
      DEFAULT endpoint ────────────────┘        qualifier="DEFAULT"    → dev / canary
      staging endpoint ──────► V3                qualifier="staging"   → pre-prod soak
      production endpoint ───► V2                qualifier="production"→ pinned, stable
python
ctl = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore-control", region_name="us-west-2")

# Promote production from V2 to V3 after soak
ctl.update_agent_runtime_endpoint(
    agentRuntimeId="my_runtime-1a2b3c4d5e",
    endpointName="production",
    agentRuntimeVersion="3",
    description="Promote V3 after 48h staging soak",
)

ctl.list_agent_runtime_versions(agentRuntimeId="my_runtime-1a2b3c4d5e")
ctl.list_agent_runtime_endpoints(agentRuntimeId="my_runtime-1a2b3c4d5e")
Recommended pattern

Point development at DEFAULT, staging at a rolling custom endpoint, and production at an explicitly pinned endpoint. Promotion becomes a single, auditable, instantly reversible API call — roll back by repointing the endpoint at the previous version.

12. Invocation & the data plane #

Service: bedrock-agentcore. Operations include InvokeAgentRuntime, InvokeAgentRuntimeWithWebSocketStream, InvokeAgentRuntimeCommand / ...CommandShell (shell execution), and StopRuntimeSession.

12.1 InvokeAgentRuntime parameters

ParameterDescription
agentRuntimeArn (req.)Runtime ARN, or agent ID plus accountId.
payload (req.)Request body blob, up to 100 MB.
runtimeSessionId33–256 characters. Drives isolation and microVM affinity.
qualifierEndpoint name, e.g. DEFAULT or production.
contentType / acceptRequest and desired response MIME types (application/json, text/event-stream).
runtimeUserIdEnd-user identifier (1–1024 chars), used for identity-aware flows.
mcpSessionId, mcpProtocolVersion, mcpMethod, mcpNameMCP-specific routing — mcpMethod is one of tools/call, resources/read, prompts/get.
traceId, traceParent, traceState, baggageDistributed-tracing propagation (X-Ray and W3C formats). baggage up to 8,192 chars.

Response fields mirror the inputs (runtimeSessionId, mcpSessionId, trace fields) plus contentType, statusCode, and response as a streaming blob.

bash
aws bedrock-agentcore invoke-agent-runtime \
  --agent-runtime-arn arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:runtime/my_runtime-1a2b3c4d5e \
  --qualifier production \
  --content-type application/json \
  --accept application/json \
  --payload '{"prompt":"Hello, agent"}' \
  --runtime-session-id "user-123456-conversation-0000000001" \
  --trace-parent "00-4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-00f067aa0ba902b7-01" \
  --baggage "tenant=acme,tier=enterprise" \
  response.json

12.2 Consuming a streaming response

python
resp = client.invoke_agent_runtime(
    agentRuntimeArn=agent_arn,
    runtimeSessionId=session_id,
    accept="text/event-stream",
    payload=json.dumps({"prompt": "Summarise this 40-page report"}).encode(),
)

for chunk in resp["response"]:                 # EventStream of bytes
    line = chunk.decode("utf-8")
    if line.startswith("data: "):
        print(json.loads(line[6:]), flush=True)
OAuth runtimes

For runtimes configured with a JWT authorizer, call the HTTPS endpoint directly with an Authorization: Bearer <token> header rather than going through the SigV4-signing AWS SDK path.

12.3 Invocation ceilings

  • Synchronous request timeout: 15 minutes (not adjustable). Longer work must run asynchronously — see §14.
  • Streaming: max 60 minutes per stream, 10 MB per chunk.
  • Asynchronous jobs: up to 8 hours (microVM).
  • WebSocket: 64 KB frames, 250 frames/sec per connection.

12.4 Shell command execution

Since March 2026, InvokeAgentRuntimeCommand executes shell commands inside a live session with HTTP/2 streaming, and interactive terminals support up to 10 concurrent shells per runtime session. This is what makes coding agents, environment-mutating agents and "install a package then use it" workflows practical.

13. Sessions & state #

13.1 Session IDs

  • Minimum 33 characters (max 256). A bare UUID4 qualifies; prefixing with tenant/user context aids debugging.
  • One ID per user per conversation. Reuse it across turns; never share it across users.
  • Generate it server-side and bind it to your authenticated user — see the warning in §4.2.
python
session_id = "user-123456-conversation-12345678"   # ≥ 33 chars

r1 = client.invoke_agent_runtime(
    agentRuntimeArn=agent_arn, runtimeSessionId=session_id,
    payload=json.dumps({"prompt": "Tell me about AWS"}).encode())

# Same session → same microVM → agent still has the conversation in memory
r2 = client.invoke_agent_runtime(
    agentRuntimeArn=agent_arn, runtimeSessionId=session_id,
    payload=json.dumps({"prompt": "How does it compare to other cloud providers?"}).encode())

13.2 Timeouts

SettingFieldDefaultRange
Idle session timeoutidleRuntimeSessionTimeout900 s (15 min)60 – 1,209,600 s
Max session lifetimemaxLifetime28,800 s (8 h)60 – 1,209,600 s

A session ends on whichever comes first: idle expiry, max lifetime, explicit StopRuntimeSession, or a failed health check. The session identity stays valid until the runtime ARN is deleted, so a later invocation on the same ID simply provisions new compute.

13.3 Three tiers of state

In-process (free)

Python objects, conversation history, caches. Survives across turns within a live session. Lost on session stop.

Session filesystem

Ephemeral by default; with managed session storage it survives stop/resume for up to 14 days, 1 GB.

External / durable

AgentCore Memory, DynamoDB, S3, EFS. The only correct place for anything that must outlive the session.

Design rule

Treat in-session state as a cache, never as the record. A session can stop at any moment — quota pressure, a health blip, a deploy. If losing it would corrupt the user's work, persist it externally at every meaningful checkpoint.

14. Asynchronous & long-running work #

The synchronous request timeout is 15 minutes, but a session lives up to 8 hours. The bridge between the two is the async-task pattern: return to the caller immediately, keep working in the background, and hold /ping at HealthyBusy so Runtime doesn't reap the session.

14.1 SDK task tracking

python
import threading, time
from strands import Agent, tool
from bedrock_agentcore.runtime import BedrockAgentCoreApp

app = BedrockAgentCoreApp()

@tool
def start_background_task(duration: int = 5) -> str:
    """Start a long-running background job and return immediately."""
    task_id = app.add_async_task("background_processing", {"duration": duration})

    def work():
        try:
            time.sleep(duration)          # real work goes here
        finally:
            app.complete_async_task(task_id)   # always complete, even on failure

    threading.Thread(target=work, daemon=True).start()
    return f"Started task {task_id}; agent status is now BUSY."

agent = Agent(tools=[start_background_task])

@app.entrypoint
def main(payload):
    return {"message": agent(str(payload.get("prompt", ""))).message}

if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run()

While at least one task is open, /ping reports HealthyBusy and the idle timer is suspended. When the last task completes, the status returns to Healthy and the normal idle clock resumes.

14.2 Rules that keep long jobs alive

  • Never block the entrypoint. A blocking call in the handler starves the health thread — the classic "killed at exactly 15 minutes" failure.
  • Always complete tasks in a finally. An orphaned task pins the session for its full 8 hours and consumes concurrency quota.
  • Don't fake activity. Refreshing time_of_last_update every ping is the documented cause of runaway session counts.
  • Checkpoint progress externally so a client can poll status on a separate short invocation, and so a session stop doesn't destroy hours of work.
  • Beyond 8 hours, either checkpoint-and-resume across sessions or move to the Instances compute type (14 days).

15. Filesystems & storage #

filesystemConfigurations attaches one or more mounts to the session's filesystem.

TypeConfig keySemantics
Managed session storagesessionStoragePer-session persistent volume surviving stop/resume. Max 1 GB, ~14-day retention.
S3 Files access points3FilesAccessPointMount an S3 dataset as a filesystem with automatic synchronisation.
Amazon EFS access pointefsAccessPointShared, durable POSIX filesystem across sessions and services.
Capacity-provider volumecapacityProviderVolumeEBS volume defined on the capacity provider (Instances).

Session storage limits

LimitValue
Maximum storage size1 GB
Filesystem metadata~50 MB (roughly 100k–200k files)
Maximum directory depth200 levels
Maximum filename length255 bytes
Maximum symlink target length4,095 bytes

Persistence is what makes an agent feel continuous: installed packages, cloned repositories, build artifacts and working files survive a stop, so resuming a session doesn't mean rebuilding the world. On Instances, co-located agents additionally share /tmp/agentcore-session/<session-id>/, enabling file-based collaboration without any API round trip.

16. Identity & authentication #

16.1 Inbound: who may invoke the agent

ModeConfigurationUse when
IAM SigV4 (default)Omit authorizerConfiguration; control access with IAM policiesCallers are AWS principals — backend services, Lambda, other agents
OAuth 2.0 / OIDC JWTauthorizerConfiguration.customJWTAuthorizerCallers are end users or SPAs authenticated by Cognito, Okta, Entra ID, Auth0…
json
{
  "customJWTAuthorizer": {
    "discoveryUrl": "https://cognito-idp.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/us-west-2_ABC123/.well-known/openid-configuration",
    "allowedClients": ["3n4b5c6d7e8f9g0h1i2j3k4l5m"],
    "allowedAudience": ["my-agent-api"],
    "allowedScopes": ["agent:invoke"],
    "customClaims": [
      {
        "inboundTokenClaimName": "cognito:groups",
        "inboundTokenClaimValueType": "STRING_ARRAY",
        "authorizingClaimMatchValue": {
          "claimMatchValue": { "matchValueStringList": ["agent-users", "admins"] },
          "claimMatchOperator": "CONTAINS_ANY"
        }
      }
    ]
  }
}

Beyond issuer and audience validation, customClaims lets you authorise on arbitrary token claims with EQUALS, CONTAINS or CONTAINS_ANY — group- or tenant-based authorisation without writing a custom authorizer. allowedWorkloadConfiguration further restricts which workload identities or hosting environments may call in.

An unauthenticated call to an OAuth runtime returns RFC 7235-compliant discovery metadata:

http
HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized
WWW-Authenticate: Bearer resource_metadata="https://bedrock-agentcore.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/runtimes/{ESCAPED_ARN}/invocations/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource?qualifier=DEFAULT"

16.2 Workload identity & outbound auth

Every runtime receives a workload identity at creation (returned as workloadIdentityDetails.workloadIdentityArn). AgentCore Identity exchanges a validated inbound user token for a workload access token that is cryptographically bound to the agent identity and the user ID — so an agent cannot obtain another user's credentials. Three token-exchange operations back this:

  • GetWorkloadAccessToken — agent identity only.
  • GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT — exchanges a verified inbound JWT. Preferred in production.
  • GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId — trusts a caller-supplied user ID with no IdP verification. Development only; deny it explicitly in production policies.

For outbound calls, AgentCore Identity's token vault stores refresh tokens and API keys for third-party providers (Slack, GitHub, Google, Zoom, custom OAuth), so agents perform authorised actions on behalf of a user without you writing an OAuth broker.

Production guardrail

Explicitly Deny bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId in production and grant only GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT. The managed policy BedrockAgentCoreFullAccess includes the unverified variant — it is a development convenience, not a production posture.

17. IAM reference #

17.1 Execution role trust policy

json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "AssumeRolePolicy",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRole",
    "Condition": {
      "StringEquals": { "aws:SourceAccount": "123456789012" },
      "ArnLike": { "aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:*" }
    }
  }]
}

The aws:SourceAccount and aws:SourceArn conditions are the confused-deputy guard. Do not drop them.

17.2 Execution role permissions policy

json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    { "Sid": "ECRImageAccess", "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["ecr:BatchGetImage", "ecr:GetDownloadUrlForLayer"],
      "Resource": ["arn:aws:ecr:us-east-1:123456789012:repository/*"] },

    { "Sid": "ECRTokenAccess", "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["ecr:GetAuthorizationToken"], "Resource": "*" },

    { "Sid": "LogsAccess", "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["logs:CreateLogGroup", "logs:CreateLogStream", "logs:PutLogEvents",
                 "logs:DescribeLogGroups", "logs:DescribeLogStreams", "logs:PutResourcePolicy"],
      "Resource": ["arn:aws:logs:us-east-1:123456789012:log-group:/aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/*"] },

    { "Sid": "XRayAccess", "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["xray:PutTraceSegments", "xray:PutTelemetryRecords",
                 "xray:GetSamplingRules", "xray:GetSamplingTargets"],
      "Resource": ["*"] },

    { "Sid": "CloudWatchMetrics", "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "cloudwatch:PutMetricData", "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": { "StringEquals": { "cloudwatch:namespace": "bedrock-agentcore" } } },

    { "Sid": "GetAgentAccessToken", "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessToken",
                 "bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT",
                 "bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default",
        "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default/workload-identity/agentName-*"
      ] },

    { "Sid": "BedrockModelInvocation", "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel", "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"],
      "Resource": ["arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/*",
                   "arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1:123456789012:*"] }
  ]
}

Add whatever else your agent code touches — S3 buckets, DynamoDB tables, Secrets Manager secrets — scoped as tightly as possible. This role is your agent's authority; a prompt-injected agent can use everything in it.

17.3 Caller permissions

json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    { "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:InvokeAgentRuntime"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:runtime/my_runtime-*",
        "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:runtime/my_runtime-*/runtime-endpoint/production"
      ] },
    { "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT"],
      "Resource": "*" },
    { "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId"],
      "Resource": "*" }
  ]
}

Deployers additionally need bedrock-agentcore:CreateAgentRuntime (and friends), iam:PassRole for the execution role, and ECR push or S3 put permissions.

18. Networking & VPC #

networkModeBehaviourUse when
PUBLICService-managed egress to the public internetAgent only calls public APIs and AWS service endpoints
VPCENIs in your subnets with your security groups; egress follows your route tablesAgent must reach RDS, OpenSearch, internal APIs, or egress must be inspected
json
{
  "networkMode": "VPC",
  "networkModeConfig": {
    "subnets": ["subnet-0a1b2c3d", "subnet-4e5f6a7b"],
    "securityGroups": ["sg-0123456789abcdef0"],
    "requireServiceS3Endpoint": true
  }
}
  • In VPC mode, outbound internet access requires a NAT gateway (or equivalent) in the route table — private subnets alone will silently break model and API calls.
  • Use interface VPC endpoints (PrivateLink) for Bedrock, ECR, CloudWatch Logs and STS to keep traffic off the internet; requireServiceS3Endpoint enforces an S3 endpoint for service traffic.
  • Use at least two subnets in different Availability Zones for resilience.
  • IAM condition keys bedrock-agentcore:Subnets and bedrock-agentcore:SecurityGroups let you mandate approved network configurations at the policy level.
  • AgentCore supports AWS PrivateLink for its own API endpoints, so the control and data planes can be reached privately.

19. Observability #

Runtime emits CloudWatch metrics out of the box; rich GenAI traces require instrumenting with the AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT).

19.1 Enable CloudWatch Transaction Search

bash
aws logs put-resource-policy --policy-name AgentCoreSpansPolicy --policy-document '{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "xray.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": "logs:PutLogEvents",
    "Resource": [
      "arn:aws:logs:us-west-2:123456789012:log-group:aws/spans:*",
      "arn:aws:logs:us-west-2:123456789012:log-group:/aws/application-signals/data:*"
    ]
  }]
}'

aws xray update-trace-segment-destination --destination CloudWatchLogs

# Optional sampling
aws xray update-indexing-rule --name "Default" \
  --rule '{"Probabilistic": {"DesiredSamplingPercentage": 10}}'

Console path: CloudWatch → Application Signals (APM) → Transaction search → Enable Transaction Search, with "ingest spans as structured logs" checked.

19.2 Instrument the agent

bash
# requirements.txt
aws-opentelemetry-distro>=0.18.0
boto3

# run under auto-instrumentation
opentelemetry-instrument python main.py
dockerfile
CMD ["opentelemetry-instrument", "python", "main.py"]

19.3 Environment variables

Hosted on AgentCore Runtime — the platform supplies the rest:

bash
OTEL_PYTHON_DISTRO=aws_distro
OTEL_PYTHON_CONFIGURATOR=aws_configurator
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=http/protobuf
OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp

Agent hosted outside AgentCore but reporting into the same panes:

bash
AGENT_OBSERVABILITY_ENABLED=true
OTEL_PYTHON_DISTRO=aws_distro
OTEL_PYTHON_CONFIGURATOR=aws_configurator
OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES=service.name=<agent-name>,aws.log.group.names=/aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/<agent-id>,cloud.resource_id=<AgentEndpointArn>
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_LOGS_HEADERS=x-aws-log-group=/aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/<agent-id>,x-aws-log-stream=runtime-logs,x-aws-metric-namespace=bedrock-agentcore
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_HEADERS=x-aws-log-group=/aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/<agent-id>,x-aws-log-stream=spans
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=http/protobuf
OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp
OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp
OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=awsemf

Two useful switches: UNIFIED_TRACES_DESTINATION_ENABLED=true opts an existing agent into per-agent span log groups instead of the shared aws/spans group (default for agents created after Jul 2026), and DISABLE_ADOT_OBSERVABILITY=true turns off ADOT when you use a different telemetry platform.

19.4 Log groups

ResourceLog group
Runtime application logs/aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/<agent_id>-<endpoint_name>/runtime-logs
Spans (unified destination)/aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/<agent_id>-<endpoint_name>, stream spans
Spans (legacy shared)aws/spans
Memory / Gateway/aws/vendedlogs/bedrock-agentcore/{resource-type}/APPLICATION_LOGS/{resource-id}

19.5 Correlating a session end-to-end

python
from opentelemetry import baggage
from opentelemetry.context import attach

ctx = baggage.set_baggage("session.id", session_id)
attach(ctx)     # every downstream span now carries session.id

Propagation headers accepted on invocation: X-Amzn-Bedrock-AgentCore-Runtime-Session-Id, X-Amzn-Trace-Id (X-Ray, e.g. Root=1-…;Parent=…;Sampled=1) and traceparent (W3C). Supported third-party instrumentation includes OpenInference, OpenLLMetry, OpenLIT and Traceloop.

19.6 What to watch

  • ActiveSessionCount (added Aug 2026) — real-time active sessions per service type. Alarm at ~70% of your quota; a steadily climbing value with flat traffic is the classic leaked-session signature.
  • Invocation count, error rate and latency, split by endpoint/qualifier.
  • Throttling (429) and quota (402) exception counts.
  • Session duration distribution — a long right tail means work isn't finishing or tasks aren't being completed.
  • Per-session token and tool-call counts from GenAI spans, for both cost and behavioural drift.

Endpoints (aliases) per agent10yes
Maximum container image size2 GBno

Direct code package — uncompressed750 MBno

Capacity providers per account1,000no
Agents per capacity-provider session20no

20.2 Invocation

LimitValueAdjustable
Synchronous request timeout15 minutesno
Maximum payload size100 MBno
Streaming chunk size10 MBno
Streaming maximum duration60 minutesno
Asynchronous job maximum duration8 hoursno

20.3 Throttling

LimitValueAdjustable
Data plane API request rate1,000 TPS (account, shared)yes
InvokeAgentRuntime per agent, per account200 TPSyes
New session creation rate25 TPS (direct code: 25 TPS/endpoint · containers: 400/min/endpoint)yes
WebSocket frame rate per connection250 frames/secno
Control plane mutation APIs50 TPSno
Control plane Get APIs150 TPSno
Control plane List APIs25 TPSno
Capacity planning

The binding constraint for most workloads is active sessions, not TPS. Concurrent sessions ≈ arrival rate × average session lifetime — and lifetime is dominated by the idle timeout, not by processing time. Cutting idleRuntimeSessionTimeout from 15 minutes to 3 for one-shot workloads can reduce concurrent session count by an order of magnitude. Call StopRuntimeSession when a conversation ends.

21. Pricing & cost model #

Verify before you budget

Rates below are the published on-demand US rates as of 18 Aug 2026 and vary by region. Always confirm against the AWS pricing page.

21.1 microVM rates

DimensionRateHow it accrues
CPU$0.0895 per vCPU-hourOnly while CPU is actually consumed — no charge during I/O wait (LLM calls, API waits)
Memory$0.00945 per GB-hourPeak memory consumed per billing increment, 128 MB floor, from boot through termination
  • Minimum billing increment: 1 second.
  • The billable window spans microVM boot, initialisation, active processing, idle time and teardown — memory accrues throughout, CPU only when used.
  • No pre-sizing: you don't choose a memory tier up front.
  • Storage is separate: ECR for images, S3 Standard for code packages, plus standard data-transfer rates.
  • New AWS customers may have up to $200 in Free Tier credits applicable to AgentCore.

21.2 Worked example

A conversational turn lasting 60 seconds wall-clock, of which ~18 seconds is real CPU work (the rest is waiting on model tokens), averaging 1 vCPU and 2 GB:

text
CPU     : 18 s  = 0.005 vCPU-h × $0.0895   ≈ $0.000448
Memory  : 60 s  = 2 GB × 0.01667 h × $0.00945 ≈ $0.000315
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Per turn                                    ≈ $0.00076
100,000 turns/month                         ≈ $76  (excluding model tokens)

Two lessons fall out of the arithmetic. First, model tokens usually dominate the bill — Runtime compute is often a rounding error beside Bedrock inference. Second, idle sessions cost memory: a 2 GB session left idle for 15 minutes costs ~$0.0047 doing nothing, which is trivial once but material across 5,000 leaked sessions.

21.3 Instances pricing

EC2 instance price plus an AgentCore management fee — 12% standard, 7.8% for GPU instances. Because the instances live in your account, Savings Plans, Reserved Instances and On-Demand Capacity Reservations apply. Stop sessions to avoid paying for idle instance time; EBS persists and continues to bill.

21.4 Cost controls

  • Tune idleRuntimeSessionTimeout to the real conversational rhythm.
  • Call StopRuntimeSession on explicit conversation end.
  • Trim container images — image size affects cold-start CPU and time.
  • Tag runtimes for cost allocation and split dev/prod into separate runtimes.
  • Alarm on ActiveSessionCount — leaked sessions are the most common surprise line item.
  • For steady, predictable, hardware-hungry load, price Instances with a Savings Plan against microVM on-demand.

22. Security best practices #

Bind sessions to authenticated users

Generate session IDs server-side, store the user↔session mapping, and authorise every invocation against it. Never let a client name its own session.

Least-privilege execution role

The execution role is the agent's blast radius. Scope to specific model ARNs, buckets and tables. Assume prompt injection will occur and ask what the injected agent could reach.

Prefer JWT over user-ID trust

Grant GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT; explicitly deny GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId outside development.

VPC mode for private data

Any agent touching internal systems belongs in VPC mode with security-group egress control and PrivateLink endpoints.

No secrets in environment variables

Use Secrets Manager or the AgentCore Identity token vault; fetch at runtime through the execution role.

Pin production to a version

A custom endpoint on an explicit version prevents an accidental UpdateAgentRuntime from reaching users, and makes rollback one call.

Numeric USER in the Dockerfile

USER 1000, not USER myuser — a named user on images with >53 layers triggers an overlay-mount failure surfacing as HTTP 424.

Keep MMDSv2 enabled

Since 30 Jun 2026, invocations require MMDSv2. Set metadataConfiguration.requireMMDSV2 = true on older runtimes.

Don't trust tool output

Retrieved documents and tool responses are untrusted input. Validate before acting, and require human approval for irreversible actions.

Log for audit, redact for privacy

Traces capture reasoning steps and tool arguments. Scrub PII/secrets before they reach spans, and set log-group retention deliberately.

Long job killed at ~15 minutes/ping returns Healthy while working, or the entrypoint blocks the ping threadUse add_async_task/complete_async_task or a custom ping; move blocking work to a thread
Session count climbs; ServiceQuotaExceededException / maxVmstime_of_last_update refreshed on every ping, so sessions never idle outEmit it only on status change (or omit); upgrade bedrock-agentcore; call StopRuntimeSession to drain stuck sessions

23.1 Debug checklist

bash
# 1. Does the image even run, and is it the right arch?
docker run --rm -p 8080:8080 <ecr-uri>
docker inspect <ecr-uri> | jq '.[0].Architecture'          # expect "arm64"

# 2. Does it satisfy the contract?
curl -s http://localhost:8080/ping
curl -s -X POST http://localhost:8080/invocations \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"prompt":"hello"}'

# 3. What did the deployed container actually say?
aws logs tail /aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/<agent-id>-DEFAULT --follow
agentcore logs --follow

# 4. Is the runtime healthy and on the version you think?
aws bedrock-agentcore-control get-agent-runtime --agent-runtime-id <id>
aws bedrock-agentcore-control list-agent-runtime-endpoints --agent-runtime-id <id>

# 5. Are sessions leaking?
#    CloudWatch → metric ActiveSessionCount → compare against your quota

Appendix C — Glossary #

TermMeaning
Agent runtimeThe deployed resource: artifact + configuration, identified by an ARN.
VersionImmutable snapshot of a runtime's full configuration. V1 at creation; a new version per update.
Endpoint (qualifier)Named pointer to a version. DEFAULT tracks latest; custom endpoints pin.
SessionAn isolated execution context keyed by runtimeSessionId, backed by one microVM (or one instance).
microVMLightweight hardware-isolated VM providing a session's CPU, memory and filesystem.
Capacity providerInfrastructure template (instance types, VPC, EBS, roles) for the Instances compute type.
Workload identityThe agent's own identity, used to obtain scoped access tokens for outbound calls.
Service contractThe HTTP endpoints and semantics a container must implement to be hostable.
MCPModel Context Protocol — JSON-RPC standard for exposing tools/resources/prompts to agents.
A2AAgent-to-Agent protocol — JSON-RPC standard for inter-agent communication, with Agent Cards.
AG-UIAgent–User Interaction protocol — event-stream standard for agent-driven UIs.
ADOTAWS Distro for OpenTelemetry — the instrumentation layer behind AgentCore Observability.
