Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory

A complete technical reference for the managed memory service for AI agents — the data model, short-term events, long-term extraction strategies, namespaces, retrieval and metadata filtering, APIs, framework integration, IAM, encryption, quotas, pricing, and troubleshooting.

Document revision: 18 August 2026  ·  Service status: Generally available (GA 13 Oct 2025)  ·  Control plane: bedrock-agentcore-control  ·  Data plane: bedrock-agentcore
Fast-moving service

Memory has shipped substantial capability since GA — episodic strategy (Dec 2025), resource-based policies and record streaming (Mar 2026), structured metadata filtering (Apr 2026), Harness managed memory GA (Jun 2026), GovCloud (Aug 2026). Every figure below reflects AWS documentation as of the revision date. Re-check quotas and pricing against the live AWS docs before designing to a hard limit.

Architect's brief — read this first #

The parts that shape architecture rather than the parts you can look up. Everything here is expanded in the reference sections that follow.

0.1 The one idea everything else follows from

Memory is two stores with opposite consistency guarantees wearing one service name.

Short-term eventsLong-term records
NatureWrite-ahead log — raw, immutable, dumbLLM-derived materialised view — distilled, smart
ConsistencySynchronous, immediately readableAsynchronous, eventually consistent (seconds → minutes)
Cost per operationCheap ($0.25 / 1,000 events)Expensive to read ($0.50 / 1,000 retrievals)
Answers“What just happened in this conversation?”“Who is this person and what do we know about them?”
The rule that falls out

Read events for recency, records for identity. Current-conversation context comes from ListEvents / get_last_k_turns — always correct, always instant. Cross-session knowledge comes from RetrieveMemoryRecords. Never write a fact and try to retrieve it as a record in the same turn: extraction is asynchronous and there is no “wait for consistency” API. Nearly every mistake with this service traces back to forgetting which store you are talking to. → §5

0.2 The decisions you cannot walk back

actorId is a one-way door

It defines what can ever be recalled together. user-123 shares memory across all your agents; {agentName}/{userId} gives each agent a private view of the same person; a tenant ID pools a whole org. Change it later and everything already written is orphaned — not deleted, just unreachable. Decide before you ship, and ask now whether a second agent will ever need to read the first one's memory. → §4

Namespaces are four things at once

Where records are filed, what retrieval searches, what IAM conditions on — and, the load-bearing one, the consolidation boundary. Records only merge within a namespace. Facts about one user split across two namespaces duplicate forever instead of merging. If memory “grows but never gets smarter,” this is almost always why. → §10

New strategies don't backfill

Strategies apply forward only. Ship with semantic, add episodic in month six, and you get episodes from month six. Replaying through CreateEvent or importing via BatchCreateMemoryRecords is the only backfill path. → §14

Two namespace traps worth stating plainly

Always terminate a namespace with /. /users/1 is a prefix of /users/10 — in a multi-tenant system that is a cross-tenant read, not a typo. And don't over-nest: every level narrows the candidate set, and five levels deep semantic search has almost nothing left to search.

0.3 What will surprise you in production

SurpriseWhy it happensWhat to do
Cost is shaped opposite to intuition — retrieval dominates, not storage $0.50 per 1,000 retrievals vs. $0.75 per 1,000 records stored per month. Two retrievals per turn (preferences + facts — the natural design) can be ~8× the storage bill. Architectural, not a knob: fetch stable things like preferences once per session and cache them in session state. Query per-turn only for what genuinely varies with the question. → §21
You'll throttle at 5 TPS, not 200 The account limit of 200 TPS lulls you; the real ceiling is 5 TPS of conversational CreateEvent per actor per session, and it is not adjustable. Batch turns into one event — up to 100 messages fit — which also cuts per-event cost proportionally. That is exactly what Strands' batch_size is for. → §20
Strategy choice is determined by agent type, not preference Semantic and user-preference read only USER/ASSISTANT roles — they are for agents that converse. Episodic reads tool results and reconstructs what the agent did and whether it worked. Building an acting agent (coding, ops, workflow) without TOOL payloads and episodic means storing the dialogue and discarding the behaviour. Purely conversational? Tool payloads just cost money — use extractionMode: SKIP. → §8
Episodic reflections generalise across actors by default A reflection namespaced at /strategy/{id}/ spans every actor. Right for “which deployment approach usually works”; wrong for anything personal. Scope reflections to /actor/{actorId}/ unless the insight is genuinely impersonal. → §8.4

0.4 Two things to be disciplined about

Memory is not a system of record

Records are LLM inferences over conversation, subject to consolidation drift. Fine for “this customer prefers window seats.” Not fine for a balance, an entitlement, a medical fact or a legal status. Use memory to know what to ask about, then fetch the authoritative value from the system that owns it.

Memory turns prompt injection from a one-turn problem into a persistent one

An injected “fact” gets extracted, consolidated, and then retrieved into every future conversation — potentially other users' conversations if namespaces leak. So: guardrails on the way in, not just on the way out; treat retrieved records as untrusted input that must not drive privileged actions unchecked; and put memory poisoning in your red-teaming (“can a user get a false fact persisted about someone else?”). → §22

Related and easy to forget: deletion is a two-store problem. A GDPR request means DeleteEvent and BatchDeleteMemoryRecords — records outlive the events they came from, and eventExpiryDuration will not touch them. Write that runbook before you need it.

0.5 When not to use it

The buy-vs-build line is consolidation. Extraction is a prompt. Embeddings are a library call. Vector search is a managed index. But “decide whether this new fact is novel, refines an existing record, or is redundant — then merge it correctly, forever, per user” is genuinely hard, and it is the thing you are paying for.

Durable history only

Configure a memory with no strategies. It becomes cheap, durable event storage — a legitimate and much cheaper design. → §5.2

Retrieval over documents

That's a Knowledge Base or your own vector store, not memory. Memory is about interactions, not a corpus. → §3.2

Write-then-immediately-read

The async extraction pipeline will fight you the whole way. Use short-term events, or reconsider the requirement.

0.6 Budget the context window deliberately

Memory that overflows the context window is worse than no memory. Give each source a token budget and assemble the prompt on purpose:

SourceGoes whereWhySuggested tuning
PreferencesSystem promptStable across the whole conversationtop_k=5, relevance_score=0.7 — few and confident
FactsSystem promptStable, but broader recall is usefultop_k=10, relevance_score=0.3
Recent turnsMessage historyImmediately consistent; needs no rankingget_last_k_turns(k), k tuned to model context
EpisodesLabelled “prior experience” blockMust be visibly distinct from the current taskQuery by intent, small top_k
Tune per namespace, never globally

A single global threshold either floods the prompt with weak facts or silently drops the preferences the user explicitly stated. retrieval_config is a map of namespace → RetrievalConfig precisely so you can set these independently. → §16.1

1. Overview #

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory is a fully managed service that gives AI agents persistent recall. It solves the defining weakness of LLM-based agents — statelessness — by storing raw conversation events, then asynchronously distilling them into durable, searchable insights that survive across sessions, deployments and even across agents.

The service splits cleanly into two halves that solve two different problems:

Short-term memory

Immutable, chronological events scoped to an actor and a session. This is the raw transcript: what was said, which tools ran, what they returned. Retrieved in order, cheaply, for turn-to-turn continuity — so "what about tomorrow?" still means Seattle.

Long-term memory

Structured memory records extracted from those events by configurable strategies, stored in hierarchical namespaces and retrieved by semantic search. This is the distillate: facts, preferences, session summaries, episodes. It is what makes an agent recognise a returning user.

365 d
Max raw event retention
5
Strategy types
6
Strategies per memory
10 MB
Max event size
100
Messages per event
150
Memories per region/account

1.1 What the service actually does for you

  • Durable storage of raw interactions with per-event expiry (7–365 days), encryption, and pagination — no DynamoDB table to design.
  • LLM-driven extraction and consolidation, run asynchronously off the critical path, so writing memory never slows a live conversation.
  • Consolidation, not just accumulation. New information is merged into existing records — add, update, or skip — rather than appended forever. This is the part that's genuinely hard to build yourself.
  • Vector search with namespace scoping, so retrieval is both semantically relevant and correctly isolated per user or tenant.
  • PII exclusion from long-term records by default, plus KMS encryption and resource-based policies.
The mental model

Think of it as a write-ahead log plus a continuously-maintained profile. CreateEvent appends to the log — always cheap, always synchronous. Strategies read the log in the background and maintain a set of durable records about the actor. Your agent reads the log for recency and the records for identity.

2. Where Memory fits in AgentCore #

ServiceResponsibilityRelationship to Memory
MemoryPersistent short- and long-term agent memory—
RuntimeServerless, session-isolated agent hostingRuntime state dies with the session (8 h / 14 d). Memory is where anything that must outlive it goes.
HarnessManaged agent loop with built-in memory (GA Jun 2026)Provisions and wires a Memory resource for you automatically — see §16.3.
GatewayAPIs, Lambda and MCP servers as governed toolsIndependent; tool results are often written to Memory as events.
IdentityWorkload identity, token vault, inbound/outbound OAuthSupplies the verified user identity that should drive your actorId.
ObservabilityGenAI traces, spans, metrics in CloudWatchMemory emits its own metrics and vended logs.
Memory is usable standalone

Nothing requires your agent to run on AgentCore Runtime. Memory is a plain AWS API — a Lambda function, an ECS service, or a laptop script can call CreateEvent and RetrieveMemoryRecords with SigV4 credentials. The pairing is natural, not mandatory.

3. When to use it #

DimensionAgentCore MemoryRuntime session stateDIY (vector DB + LLM pipeline)Plain DynamoDB / S3
Survives session endYesNo (8 h microVM / 14 d instance)YesYes
Extraction of insightsManaged, LLM-drivenNoneYou build and operate itNone
Consolidation / dedupManaged (add / update / skip)n/aThe hardest part to build welln/a
Semantic retrievalBuilt in, namespace-scopedn/aYes, self-operatedNo
Multi-tenant isolationNamespaces + IAM conditions + RBPPer-session microVMYou build itYou build it
Operational burdenVery lowNoneHigh (index, embeddings, prompts, evals)Low
Cost shapePer event, per record stored, per retrievalIncluded in computeInfrastructure + model tokensStorage + RCU/WCU

3.1 Good fits

  • Customer-facing assistants that must recognise a returning user and their preferences.
  • Support agents that need the history of a case across many conversations.
  • Coding or workflow agents that should learn which approaches worked before (episodic strategy).
  • Multi-agent systems sharing a common view of a customer, project or inventory.
  • Anywhere you were about to build "extract facts with an LLM, embed them, dedupe them, store them per user" yourself.

3.2 Poor fits

  • A document knowledge base — that's retrieval over a corpus, not memory of interactions. Use a Bedrock Knowledge Base or your own vector store.
  • High-volume telemetry or analytics events; use a log/analytics pipeline.
  • Transactional records of authority (orders, payments, ledgers). Memory is an inference-derived store — treat it as advisory, not as a system of record.
  • Sub-second write-then-immediately-read-the-insight flows: long-term extraction is asynchronous by design.

4. Data model & core concepts #

 Memory resource  (mem-abc123…)      ← encryption, retention, strategies, indexed keys
 │
 ├─ actorId "customer-456"           ← the entity memory is about (user, agent, or pair)
 │   ├─ sessionId "sess-789"         ← one conversation / interaction period
 │   │   ├─ event evt-001  ─┐
 │   │   ├─ event evt-002   │  SHORT-TERM: immutable, chronological, TTL 7–365 days
 │   │   └─ event evt-003  ─┘
 │   │        │
 │   │        │  strategies run asynchronously: extract → consolidate → (reflect)
 │   │        ▼
 │   └─ LONG-TERM memory records, filed into namespaces:
 │        /strategy/{strategyId}/actor/customer-456/                 ← facts, preferences
 │        /strategy/{strategyId}/actor/customer-456/session/sess-789/ ← summaries, episodes
 │
 └─ actorId "customer-457" …          ← fully separate namespace subtree
ConceptDefinitionKey constraints
Memory resourceThe top-level container. Holds encryption config, event retention, strategy definitions and indexed keys.ID pattern <name>-<10 chars>; 150 per region per account.
ActorThe entity the memory is about — an end user, an agent, or an agent/user pair. The primary isolation boundary.actorId 1–255 chars; pattern allows / and : for hierarchy, e.g. /agent-support-123/customer-456.
SessionOne conversation or interaction period. Groups related events chronologically.sessionId 1–100 chars, alphanumeric plus - and _.
EventAn immutable record of something that happened: messages, tool calls, or a binary blob.≤100 payload items; ≤100 KB per message; ≤10 MB per event.
BranchAn alternate line of events forking from a root event — for message edits and what-if paths.branch.name + branch.rootEventId.
StrategyThe rule that turns events into long-term records: semantic, summary, user preference, episodic, or custom.6 per memory (hard); 900 per account (adjustable).
Memory recordAn extracted, consolidated insight with content, namespaces, metadata and an embedding.Created by strategies or by BatchCreateMemoryRecords.
NamespaceA slash-delimited path that files records and scopes retrieval and IAM.Templates support {actorId}, {sessionId}, {memoryStrategyId}.
Actor design is a one-way door

actorId determines what can ever be recalled together. Choosing a raw user ID gives you per-user memory; choosing {agentName}/{userId} gives each agent its own private view of the same person; choosing a tenant ID pools everyone in an organisation. Changing it later effectively orphans everything already written under the old key. Decide before you ship.

5. The memory pipeline #

  agent turn
      │
      ▼
 ┌──────────────┐   synchronous, always fast
 │ CreateEvent  │──────────────────────────────►  SHORT-TERM store (raw, immutable, TTL)
 └──────┬───────┘                                        │
        │                                                │ ListEvents / get_last_k_turns
        │ triggers (per strategy)                        ▼
        │                                        conversation context for the next turn
        ▼
 ╔═══════════════════════ ASYNCHRONOUS ═══════════════════════╗
 ║  1. EXTRACTION      LLM reads new events (+ historical      ║
 ║                     window) and proposes candidate records  ║
 ║                                                             ║
 ║  2. CONSOLIDATION   LLM compares candidates against         ║
 ║                     existing records in the namespace and   ║
 ║                     decides: AddMemory / UpdateMemory /     ║
 ║                     SkipMemory                              ║
 ║                                                             ║
 ║  3. REFLECTION      (episodic only) generalises insights    ║
 ║                     across many episodes                    ║
 ╚═══════════════════════════════╤═════════════════════════════╝
                                 ▼
                        LONG-TERM records in namespaces
                                 │
                                 ▼
              RetrieveMemoryRecords  (semantic search + metadata filters)
                                 │
                                 ▼
                    injected into the agent's prompt

5.1 Why extraction is asynchronous

Extraction and consolidation are LLM calls — seconds of latency and real token cost. Running them inline would tax every turn. Instead CreateEvent returns as soon as the raw event is durable, and strategies process in the background. The practical consequence: a fact stated in this turn may not be retrievable as a long-term record for some seconds to minutes. Design for that.

The standard workaround

Read short-term events for the current session and long-term records for everything older. The raw transcript is immediately consistent, so recent context is never missing — you simply don't depend on the distillate for things said moments ago.

5.2 Three ways to control extraction

ApproachWhat you controlCost profileEffort
Built-in strategyNothing but the namespace — AWS supplies prompts, models and schemaHighest storage rate; no model charges in your accountMinutes
Built-in with overrideAppend to the extraction/consolidation prompt and choose the Bedrock modelLower storage rate; model tokens billed to youHours
Self-managedEverything — triggers, models, algorithms, schema, external systemsLowest storage rate; you pay all compute and model costDays; you operate a pipeline

If no strategy is configured, the memory resource stores short-term events only — no long-term records are ever extracted. That is a legitimate and cheap configuration if all you need is durable conversation history.

6. Short-term memory #

6.1 CreateEvent

POST /memories/{memoryId}/events — writes an immutable event and, if strategies exist, kicks off extraction.

ParameterReq.Constraints & notes
memoryId✔Path param. Memory ID or full ARN.
actorId✔1–255 chars. Pattern permits / and :, so hierarchical actor keys are legal.
eventTimestamp✔Epoch milliseconds. Defaults to now if omitted.
payload✔0–100 items. Each item is conversational or blob.
sessionId—1–100 chars. Omitting it writes an actor-scoped event outside any session.
branch—{name, rootEventId} — see §7.
metadata—0–15 key/value entries. Drives STRICTLY_CONSISTENT metadata and extraction partitioning.
clientToken—Idempotency key. Duplicate requests are ignored without error.
extractionMode—SKIP stores the event in short-term memory only, bypassing long-term extraction.
python
import boto3, time

dp = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore", region_name="us-west-2")

dp.create_event(
    memoryId="CustomerSupportMemory-a1b2c3d4e5",
    actorId="customer-456",
    sessionId="session-789",
    eventTimestamp=int(time.time() * 1000),
    payload=[
        {"conversational": {"role": "USER",
                            "content": {"text": "Looking for a waterproof action camera"}}},
        {"conversational": {"role": "ASSISTANT",
                            "content": {"text": "How deep do you plan to dive?"}}},
    ],
    metadata={"department": {"stringValue": "retail"}},
    clientToken="turn-000042",
)

6.2 Payload types

TypeShapeUsed for extraction?
conversational{role, content:{text}} — roles include USER, ASSISTANT, TOOL, OTHERYes. Note that semantic and user-preference strategies process only USER and ASSISTANT roles; the episodic strategy benefits from TOOL results.
blobArbitrary binary or JSON — checkpoints, state snapshots, artefactsNo. Ignored by extraction. Useful for agent state you want durable but not summarised.
Write tool results if you use episodic memory

The episodic strategy reconstructs what the agent did and whether it worked. Without TOOL payloads it can see the conversation but not the actions, and episode quality collapses. Conversely, if you only use semantic/user-preference strategies, tool payloads add cost without benefit — consider extractionMode: SKIP for pure-telemetry events.

6.3 Reading events back

OperationPurposeNotable parameters
ListEventsPaginated chronological history for an actor/sessionfilter, includePayloads (default true), maxResults
GetEventFetch one event by IDeventId
DeleteEventRemove a single event (e.g. a GDPR erasure or a bad turn)Independent of eventExpiryDuration auto-deletion
ListActors / ListSessionsEnumerate actors in a memory, or sessions for an actorUseful for admin tooling and cleanup jobs
python
from bedrock_agentcore.memory import MemorySessionManager
from bedrock_agentcore.memory.constants import ConversationalMessage, MessageRole

mgr = MemorySessionManager(memory_id=MEMORY_ID, region_name="us-west-2")
session = mgr.create_memory_session(actor_id="User1", session_id="OrderSupportSession1")

session.add_turns(messages=[
    ConversationalMessage("Hi, how can I help you today?", MessageRole.ASSISTANT)])
session.add_turns(messages=[
    ConversationalMessage("My order #35476 hasn't arrived.", MessageRole.USER)])

# Rebuild recent context for the next prompt
for turn in session.get_last_k_turns(k=5):
    print(turn)

6.4 Retention

eventExpiryDuration (days) is applied per event at write time. Updating it on the memory resource affects only new events — previously stored events keep their original expiry. Plan retention before you accumulate history, and use DeleteEvent for targeted erasure.

7. Branching & checkpoints #

Conversations are not always linear. A user edits an earlier message; an agent explores two competing plans; a workflow needs to roll back. Branches model this by forking the event stream from a designated root event.

json
{
  "memoryId": "mem-12345abcdef",
  "actorId": "customer-456",
  "sessionId": "session-789",
  "eventTimestamp": 1755500000000,
  "branch": { "name": "edited-conversation", "rootEventId": "evt-67890" },
  "payload": [
    { "conversational": { "role": "USER",
        "content": { "text": "Actually, make it a 10-day trip, not 5." } } }
  ]
}

Common uses:

  • Message editing — the user rewrites a prompt; the new line branches from the message before it, and the abandoned path stays auditable.
  • What-if exploration — evaluate multiple strategies in parallel without polluting the main thread.
  • Checkpointing — write a blob payload with serialised agent state, then resume from it in a later session without replaying every turn.

8. Built-in strategies #

A strategy is a system prompt (instructions + output schema) plus a namespace template plus a place in the extract/consolidate/reflect pipeline. Multiple strategies can coexist on one memory and all run against the same event stream.

StrategyAPI fieldExtractsOutputDefault namespace
SemanticsemanticMemoryStrategyFacts and knowledge — entities, events, identifiers, specificationsJSON facts, one standalone fact per object/strategy/{memoryStrategyId}/actors/{actorId}/
SummarysummaryMemoryStrategyCondensed session narrative — topics, tasks, decisionsXML with one <topic> per area; a session may hold several summary chunks/strategy/{memoryStrategyId}/actor/{actorId}/session/{sessionId}/
User preferenceuserPreferenceMemoryStrategyChoices, styles, tone, recurring decisionsJSON with context, preference, categories/strategy/{memoryStrategyId}/actors/{actorId}/
EpisodicepisodicMemoryStrategyCompleted interaction episodes plus cross-episode reflectionsXML episodes (situation, intent, assessment, justification, reflection)/strategy/{id}/, …/actor/{actorId}/, or …/actor/{actorId}/session/{sessionId}/
CustomcustomMemoryStrategyWhatever you define — override a built-in or run your own pipelineYour schemaYours

8.1 Semantic

The workhorse. Captures durable facts: "order #XYZ-123 relates to this support case", "the project deadline is 25 October", "the customer runs version 2.1". Extraction identifies candidate facts; consolidation decides whether each is new, an update to an existing fact, or redundant. Only USER and ASSISTANT messages are processed.

8.2 Summary

Session-scoped, and the only strategy that requires {sessionId} in its namespace. Produces a rolling XML summary of the conversation, letting an agent recover the thread of a long dialogue without replaying every turn — the direct answer to context-window pressure. A long session accumulates multiple summary chunks that together form the whole.

8.3 User preference

Builds an evolving profile: preferred airline and seat type, formal vs. informal tone, favoured programming language, shipping carrier. The context field is what makes these usable — "prefers window seats on long-haul flights" is actionable in a way that "prefers window seats" is not.

8.4 Episodic

The most sophisticated of the four, and the one aimed at agents that act rather than merely converse. It records completed episodes with situation, intent, assessment and justification, and then reflects across many episodes to surface higher-order patterns: which tool combinations succeed, which failure modes recur, what the best practice turned out to be.

StepWhat happens
ExtractionAnalyses an in-progress episode and decides whether it has completed.
ConsolidationMerges the extractions into a single episode record.
ReflectionGenerates insights across episodes, written to reflectionConfiguration.namespaceTemplates.

Practical notes: episodes are only emitted once completion is detected, so incomplete work takes longer to appear. Records are indexed by intent (episodes) and use case (reflections) — phrase retrieval queries accordingly. Include TOOL payloads in your events.

Reflection privacy

Reflections placed at /strategy/{id}/ generalise across all actors. That is exactly what you want for "which deployment strategy usually works" and exactly what you must not do with personal data. Scope reflections to …/actor/{actorId}/ unless the insight is genuinely impersonal, and apply guardrails either way.

9. Overrides & self-managed strategies #

9.1 Built-in with override

Keep AWS's managed extraction pipeline and output schema, but append your own instructions and choose the model. Available for semantic, summary, user preference and episodic (including the reflection step).

python
CUSTOM_EXTRACTION = """\
Extract the user's preferred airline carrier from the choices they make.
Extract the user's preferred seat type (aisle, middle, or window).
Ignore all other preference types.
"""

CUSTOM_CONSOLIDATION = """\
For each new memory choose exactly one action:
  AddMemory    - an entirely new preference not present in existing records
  UpdateMemory - refines or enhances an existing preference
  SkipMemory   - irrelevant or duplicate
"""

strategy = {
    "customMemoryStrategy": {
        "name": "FlightPreferences",
        "namespaceTemplates": ["/preferences/{actorId}/travel/"],
        "configuration": {
            "userPreferenceOverride": {
                "extraction": {
                    "appendToPrompt": CUSTOM_EXTRACTION,
                    "modelId": "anthropic.claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022-v2:0",
                },
                "consolidation": {
                    "appendToPrompt": CUSTOM_CONSOLIDATION,
                    "modelId": "anthropic.claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022-v2:0",
                },
            }
        },
    }
}
  • appendToPrompt supplements the default system prompt while preserving the strategy's output schema. Max 30 KB.
  • modelId selects the Bedrock model invoked for that step — billed in your account, which is why the storage rate drops.
  • Requires memoryExecutionRoleArn with bedrock:InvokeModel and bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream.
  • Override keys available: semanticOverride, summaryOverride (consolidation only), userPreferenceOverride, episodicOverride (extraction, consolidation, reflection).

9.2 Self-managed

You own the entire pipeline. AgentCore watches the event stream, fires a trigger, drops the conversation payload in S3 and notifies you via SNS. You extract, consolidate and write records back through the batch APIs.

  CreateEvent ──► short-term store
                        │
                        │ trigger fires (messages / tokens / idle time)
                        ▼
      ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │ AgentCore writes payload.json to your S3    │
      │ bucket and publishes a job to your SNS topic│
      └───────────────┬─────────────────────────────┘
                      ▼
      your consumer (Lambda / ECS / Step Functions)
         1. read payload from S3
         2. extract with any model you like
         3. consolidate / dedupe against existing records
         4. BatchCreateMemoryRecords / BatchUpdate / BatchDelete
                      │
                      ▼
      long-term records land in your namespaces, fully searchable
json
{
  "customMemoryStrategy": {
    "name": "SelfManagedExtraction",
    "namespaceTemplates": ["/custom/{actorId}/"],
    "configuration": {
      "selfManagedConfiguration": {
        "triggerConditions": [
          { "messageBasedTrigger": { "messageCount": 6 } },
          { "tokenBasedTrigger":   { "tokenCount": 1000 } },
          { "timeBasedTrigger":    { "idleSessionTimeout": 30 } }
        ],
        "historicalContextWindowSize": 2,
        "invocationConfiguration": {
          "payloadDeliveryBucketName": "my-agentcore-payloads",
          "topicArn": "arn:aws:sns:us-east-1:123456789012:agentcore-memory-jobs"
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

The SNS notification carries the job pointer; the S3 object carries the conversation:

json
// SNS message
{ "jobId": "…", "s3PayloadLocation": "s3://bucket/path/payload.json",
  "memoryId": "…", "strategyId": "…" }

// S3 payload.json
{
  "requestId": "…", "accountId": "123456789012",
  "memoryId": "…", "actorId": "user-1", "sessionId": "sess-1", "strategyId": "…",
  "startingTimestamp": 1634567890, "endingTimestamp": 1634567920,
  "currentContext":    [ { "role": "USER",      "content": { "text": "…" } } ],
  "historicalContext": [ { "role": "ASSISTANT", "content": { "text": "…" } } ]
}
Operational reality

The long-term-memory SLA becomes shared — AWS delivers the trigger, you deliver the record. Budget for retries and a DLQ, put a lifecycle policy on the payload bucket, and use FIFO SNS topics where ordering matters (summarisation in particular). Deduplicate aggressively: without consolidation, self-managed memories grow without bound and retrieval quality decays.

10. Namespaces & design patterns #

A namespace is a forward-slash path with a trailing slash. It is simultaneously the filing system, the retrieval scope, the consolidation boundary and an IAM condition key — which makes namespace design the single highest-leverage decision in a Memory deployment.

GranularityTemplateScopeTypical use
Session/strategy/{memoryStrategyId}/actor/{actorId}/session/{sessionId}/One conversation, one actorSummaries, episodes
Actor/strategy/{memoryStrategyId}/actor/{actorId}/One actor, all sessionsFacts, preferences, reflections
Strategy/strategy/{memoryStrategyId}/All actors under one strategyImpersonal, shared knowledge
Global/EverythingAdmin tooling, exports

10.1 Patterns that work

Per-actor accumulation

/actor/{actorId}/facts/ and /actor/{actorId}/preferences/. Consolidation merges within a namespace, so this is what lets a profile improve across conversations instead of fragmenting.

Session-scoped summaries

/actor/{actorId}/session/{sessionId}/summary/. Keeps each conversation's narrative distinct while remaining easy to enumerate per user.

Episodes with reflection hierarchy

Episodes at /actor/{actorId}/session/{sessionId}/episodes/, reflections at /actor/{actorId}/ — the reflection namespace must be a parent path of the episode namespace.

Inverted for shared knowledge

/customer-issues/{actorId}/ lets an admin query all of /customer-issues/ while each user's own agent stays scoped to their leaf.

10.2 Anti-patterns

  • Omitting the trailing slash. /users/1 is a prefix of /users/10 — in a multi-tenant system that is a cross-tenant read.
  • Over-nesting. Every level narrows retrieval; five levels deep and semantic search has almost nothing to search.
  • Mixing isolation concerns — user data and agent-internal data in one subtree makes both IAM scoping and deletion painful.
  • Relying on metadata filters alone for tenant isolation. Namespaces are the boundary; metadata is a refinement within it.

10.3 Enforcing isolation with IAM

json
{
  "Sid": "SpecificNamespaceAccess",
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:RetrieveMemoryRecords"],
  "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:memory/CustomerSupportMemory-a1b2c3d4e5",
  "Condition": {
    "StringEquals": { "bedrock-agentcore:namespace": "summaries/agent1/" }
  }
}

Since March 2026, Memory also supports resource-based policies, so you can grant cross-account or cross-team access on the memory resource itself without editing every caller's role.

11. Retrieval APIs #

OperationMechanismUse when
RetrieveMemoryRecordsVector semantic search within a namespace, optional metadata pre-filterThe main path — "what do we know that's relevant to this question?"
ListMemoryRecordsEnumeration by namespace, optional metadata filters, no semantic rankingShow me everything in this namespace; admin views; audits
GetMemoryRecordFetch one record by IDFollow-up after a search or a stream event
ListEventsChronological raw eventsRecent context for the current turn
python
resp = dp.retrieve_memory_records(
    memoryId=MEMORY_ID,
    namespace="/preferences/customer-456/",       # exact match
    # namespacePath="/preferences/",              # OR prefix match across the subtree
    searchCriteria={
        "searchQuery": "Which camera should I recommend?",   # up to 10,000 chars
        "topK": 5,                                            # default 10, max 100
        "memoryStrategyId": "FlightPreferences-abc123",       # optional filter
    },
)

for r in resp["memoryRecords"]:
    print(round(r["score"], 3), r["content"])

Response shape:

json
{
  "memoryRecords": [
    { "memoryRecordId": "rec-…", "content": "Prefers window seats on long-haul flights",
      "score": 0.85, "namespace": ["/preferences/customer-456/"], "metadata": { } }
  ],
  "nextToken": "…"
}
Reading the score

score is a cosine similarity between embeddings, not a probability or a percentage. Absolute values are not comparable across different query phrasings or embedding spaces — calibrate a threshold empirically for your corpus. A common starting point is 0.2–0.3 for broad recall and 0.6–0.7 when precision matters more than coverage.

11.1 A workable retrieval recipe

  1. Recent context — ListEvents or get_last_k_turns(k) for the current session. Immediately consistent, no ranking needed.
  2. Identity context — RetrieveMemoryRecords against the actor-scoped preferences and facts namespaces with a small topK and a relatively high threshold.
  3. Task context — for action-taking agents, query the episodic namespace with the intent of the current task.
  4. Assemble — put preferences and facts in the system prompt (they are stable), recent turns in the message history, and episodes in a clearly labelled "prior experience" block.
  5. Cap it — memory that overflows the context window is worse than no memory. Budget tokens per source and truncate deliberately.

12. Structured metadata & filtering #

Added April 2026. Semantic search alone returns everything similar; metadata filters let you also require attributes — priority, department, tenant, time range — narrowing the candidate set before vector search runs.

12.1 Indexed keys (memory level)

json
{
  "indexedKeys": [
    { "key": "priority",       "type": "STRING" },
    { "key": "tags",           "type": "STRINGLIST" },
    { "key": "severity_score", "type": "NUMBER" }
  ]
}

Declared at CreateMemory or added later with UpdateMemory. Max 10 per memory, cannot be removed once added, and are not backfilled onto existing records. Only indexed keys are filterable.

12.2 Metadata schema (strategy level)

Defines what the LLM extracts onto each record. Only keys present in the schema are populated during event-driven extraction.

json
{
  "key": "priority",
  "type": "STRING",
  "extractionConfig": {
    "llmExtractionConfig": {
      "definition": "Issue priority by customer impact: critical (production down), high (degraded), medium (feature request), low (docs).",
      "llmExtractionInstruction": "LATEST_VALUE",
      "validation": {
        "stringValidation": { "allowedValues": ["critical", "high", "medium", "low"] }
      }
    }
  }
}
Extraction typeBehaviourConstraintsUse for
LLM_INFERRED (default)The model infers the value from conversation contentNeeds a definition; validation strongly recommendedSentiment, topic, priority, agent tier — anything judged from context
STRICTLY_CONSISTENTValue propagates verbatim from event metadata; no inferenceMust be STRING, must be an indexed key, no extractionConfig, max 3 per strategyTenant, department, compliance level — classifiers known at write time
The underrated feature

STRICTLY_CONSISTENT keys partition extraction and consolidation. Events with different values are processed separately and their records never merge. That makes it the clean way to guarantee, say, that a hipaa record and a standard record can never be consolidated into one — a structural guarantee rather than a prompt-level hope.

12.3 Filter syntax

Every filter is {left, operator, right}; up to 5 per query, combined with AND.

bash
aws bedrock-agentcore retrieve-memory-records \
  --memory-id "$MEMORY_ID" \
  --namespace "support/customer-123" \
  --search-criteria '{
    "searchQuery": "billing issues",
    "topK": 10,
    "metadataFilters": [
      { "left": {"metadataKey": "priority"},
        "operator": "EQUALS_TO",
        "right": {"metadataValue": {"stringValue": "high"}} },
      { "left": {"metadataKey": "x-amz-agentcore-memory-createdAt"},
        "operator": "AFTER",
        "right": {"metadataValue": {"dateTimeValue": "2026-01-01T00:00:00Z"}} }
    ]
  }'
OperatorTypesRight value
EQUALS_TOSTRING, NUMBERrequired
CONTAINSSTRINGLISTrequired
EXISTS / NOT_EXISTSallnot required
GREATER_THAN, GREATER_THAN_OR_EQUALS, LESS_THAN, LESS_THAN_OR_EQUALSNUMBERrequired
BEFORE / AFTERdateTimeValuerequired

System-generated metadata (always filterable)

FieldType
x-amz-agentcore-memory-recordTypestringValue
x-amz-agentcore-memory-createdAtdateTimeValue (UTC)
x-amz-agentcore-memory-updatedAtdateTimeValue (UTC)

12.4 Metadata quotas

LimitValue
Indexed keys per memory10
STRICTLY_CONSISTENT keys per strategy3
Metadata schema entries per strategy20
User-supplied metadata entries per record20
Metadata entries per CreateEvent15
Filters per query5
allowedValues per validation10
maxItems for STRINGLIST5
Metadata key length / stringValue / STRINGLIST member128 / 256 / 64 chars
Avoid

High-cardinality free text (descriptions, full names) as indexed keys; metadata for values that change every interaction; and LLM inference for values that must be exact — use STRICTLY_CONSISTENT or the batch APIs instead.

13. Batch APIs & record streaming #

13.1 Batch record APIs

BatchCreateMemoryRecords, BatchUpdateMemoryRecords and BatchDeleteMemoryRecords write long-term records directly, bypassing extraction. Two main uses: the write-back step of a self-managed strategy, and bulk import of profile data you already hold.

bash
aws bedrock-agentcore batch-create-memory-records \
  --memory-id "$MEMORY_ID" \
  --records '[{
    "requestIdentifier": "import-001",
    "namespaces": ["support/customer-456"],
    "memoryStrategyId": "SupportSemanticStrategy-abc123",
    "content": { "text": "Billing dispute resolved after account credit" },
    "metadata": {
      "priority":   { "stringValue": "medium" },
      "department": { "stringValue": "billing" }
    }
  }]'

With memoryStrategyId, only schema-defined metadata keys are retained. Without it, all metadata is stored as-is but only indexed keys remain filterable.

13.2 Memory record streaming (Kinesis)

Since March 2026, Memory can push record lifecycle events to a Kinesis Data Stream — no polling.

bash
aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-memory \
  --name "MyStreamingMemory" \
  --memory-execution-role-arn "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/AgentCoreMemoryRole" \
  --stream-delivery-resources '{
    "resources": [{
      "kinesis": {
        "dataStreamArn": "arn:aws:kinesis:us-west-2:123456789012:stream/memory-records",
        "contentConfigurations": [{ "type": "MEMORY_RECORDS", "level": "FULL_CONTENT" }]
      }
    }]
  }'
LevelContentsTrade-off
METADATA_ONLYIDs, namespaces, timestamps, strategy infoCheap notifications; requires a follow-up API call for content
FULL_CONTENTAll of the above plus memoryRecordTextSelf-contained downstream processing; larger records, and record text leaves the service boundary
json
{
  "memoryStreamEvent": {
    "eventType": "MemoryRecordCreated",
    "eventTime": "2026-03-06T16:45:00.000Z",
    "memoryId": "…", "memoryRecordId": "…",
    "namespaces": ["/preferences/customer-456/"],
    "createdAt": 1736622300000,
    "memoryStrategyId": "…", "memoryStrategyType": "…",
    "metadata": { },
    "memoryRecordText": "…"        // FULL_CONTENT only
  }
}

Event types: MemoryRecordCreated (extraction or batch create), MemoryRecordUpdated (batch update), MemoryRecordDeleted (batch/single delete or consolidation). Typical uses: data-lake replication, downstream workflow triggers, real-time profile services, and audit trails. Publishing health is visible via the StreamPublishingSuccess, StreamPublishingFailure and StreamUserError metrics.

14. CreateMemory reference #

ParameterReq.Notes
name✔Unique within the account. The memory ID becomes <name>-<10 random chars>.
eventExpiryDuration✔Days of raw-event retention. 7–365 per service quotas. Applied per event at write time.
description—Free text.
memoryStrategies—Up to 6. Omit for a short-term-only memory.
memoryExecutionRoleArn—Required for overrides, self-managed strategies and streaming — this is the role Memory assumes to call Bedrock/SNS/S3/Kinesis.
encryptionKeyArn—Customer-managed KMS key. Defaults to a service-owned key.
indexedKeys—Up to 10 filterable metadata keys. Additive only.
streamDeliveryResources—Kinesis stream targets for record lifecycle events.
clientToken—Idempotency.
tags—Cost allocation and ABAC.

14.1 A fully-configured example

python
import boto3, time

ctl = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore-control", region_name="us-west-2")

resp = ctl.create_memory(
    name="ComprehensiveAgentMemory",
    description="Facts, preferences, summaries and episodes for the support assistant",
    eventExpiryDuration=90,
    memoryExecutionRoleArn="arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/AgentCoreMemoryRole",
    encryptionKeyArn="arn:aws:kms:us-west-2:123456789012:key/abcd-1234",
    indexedKeys=[
        {"key": "priority",   "type": "STRING"},
        {"key": "department", "type": "STRING"},
        {"key": "tags",       "type": "STRINGLIST"},
    ],
    memoryStrategies=[
        {"semanticMemoryStrategy": {
            "name": "FactExtractor",
            "namespaceTemplates": ["/facts/{actorId}/"]}},
        {"userPreferenceMemoryStrategy": {
            "name": "PreferenceLearner",
            "namespaceTemplates": ["/preferences/{actorId}/"]}},
        {"summaryMemoryStrategy": {
            "name": "SessionSummarizer",
            "namespaceTemplates": ["/summaries/{actorId}/{sessionId}/"]}},
        {"episodicMemoryStrategy": {
            "name": "TaskEpisodes",
            "namespaceTemplates": ["/episodes/{actorId}/{sessionId}/"],
            "reflectionConfiguration": {
                "namespaceTemplates": ["/episodes/{actorId}/"]}}},
    ],
)

memory_id = resp["memory"]["id"]

while True:                                   # wait for ACTIVE before writing events
    status = ctl.get_memory(memoryId=memory_id)["memory"]["status"]
    if status == "ACTIVE":
        break
    if status == "FAILED":
        raise RuntimeError("Memory creation failed")
    time.sleep(10)

print("ready:", memory_id)
Updating strategies later

UpdateMemory accepts addMemoryStrategies, modifyMemoryStrategies and deleteMemoryStrategies, plus addIndexedKeys. Adding a strategy does not retroactively process historical events — new strategies apply from that point forward. If you need history reprocessed, replay it through CreateEvent or import records with the batch APIs.

15. Quickstart #

15.1 Prerequisites

bash
pip install bedrock-agentcore strands-agents
npm install -g @aws/agentcore            # AgentCore CLI (replaces the legacy Python starter toolkit)
# boto3 >= 1.39.8 / botocore >= 1.33.8 if calling the APIs directly

15.2 Create a memory with the CLI

bash
agentcore create --name agentcore-memory-quickstart --no-agent
cd agentcore-memory-quickstart

# short-term only
agentcore add memory --name my_agent_memory

# or with long-term strategies
agentcore add memory --name CustomerSupportMemory --strategies SEMANTIC,SUMMARIZATION,USER_PREFERENCE

agentcore deploy
agentcore status                          # shows the memory ARN / ID

15.3 Write and read

python
import boto3
from bedrock_agentcore.memory import MemorySessionManager
from bedrock_agentcore.memory.constants import ConversationalMessage, MessageRole

ctl = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore-control", region_name="us-west-2")
memory_id = ctl.list_memories()["memories"][0]["id"]

mgr = MemorySessionManager(memory_id=memory_id, region_name="us-west-2")
session = mgr.create_memory_session(actor_id="User1", session_id="OrderSupportSession1")

session.add_turns(messages=[ConversationalMessage(
    "Hi, how can I help you today?", MessageRole.ASSISTANT)])
session.add_turns(messages=[ConversationalMessage(
    "I'm a new customer. I ordered #35476 and it hasn't arrived.", MessageRole.USER)])
session.add_turns(messages=[ConversationalMessage(
    "I'm sorry to hear that. Let me look up your order.", MessageRole.ASSISTANT)])

# short-term: chronological, immediately consistent
for turn in session.get_last_k_turns(k=5):
    print("TURN:", turn)

# long-term: available once asynchronous extraction has run
for rec in session.list_long_term_memory_records(namespace_path="/"):
    print("RECORD:", rec)

hits = session.search_long_term_memories(
    query="can you summarize the support issue",
    namespace_path="/",
    top_k=3,
)

15.4 Clean up

bash
agentcore remove memory --name CustomerSupportMemory
agentcore deploy

16. Framework integration #

16.1 Strands: AgentCoreMemorySessionManager

The cleanest integration. Attach a session manager to a Strands Agent and memory reads and writes happen automatically around every turn — no manual CreateEvent calls, no retrieval plumbing.

python
from datetime import datetime
from strands import Agent
from bedrock_agentcore.memory.integrations.strands.config import (
    AgentCoreMemoryConfig, RetrievalConfig)
from bedrock_agentcore.memory.integrations.strands.session_manager import (
    AgentCoreMemorySessionManager)

config = AgentCoreMemoryConfig(
    memory_id=MEMORY_ID,
    actor_id="customer-456",
    session_id=f"session_{datetime.now():%Y%m%d%H%M%S}",
    batch_size=10,                       # buffer turns before flushing to CreateEvent
    retrieval_config={
        "/preferences/{actorId}/": RetrievalConfig(top_k=5,  relevance_score=0.7),
        "/facts/{actorId}/":       RetrievalConfig(top_k=10, relevance_score=0.3),
    },
)

with AgentCoreMemorySessionManager(config, region_name="us-west-2") as session_manager:
    agent = Agent(
        system_prompt="You are a helpful assistant. Use what you know about the user.",
        session_manager=session_manager,
    )
    agent("I like sushi with tuna")
    agent("I like pizza")
    agent("What should I buy for lunch today?")   # recalls both preferences
# exiting the context manager flushes any buffered turns
ObjectFieldMeaning
AgentCoreMemoryConfigmemory_idTarget memory resource (required)
actor_idWhose memory this is (required)
session_idThis conversation (required)
batch_sizeTurns buffered before writing; default 1. Higher values cut API calls and cost
retrieval_configMap of namespace template → RetrievalConfig
RetrievalConfigtop_kRecords to return, 1–1000 (default 10)
relevance_scoreMinimum similarity, 0.0–1.0 (default 0.2)
strategy_idRestrict to one strategy's records
Tune per namespace, not globally

Preferences should be few and confident (top_k=5, relevance_score=0.7); facts should be broader (top_k=10, relevance_score=0.3). One global threshold either floods the prompt with weak facts or silently drops the user's stated preferences.

16.2 Other frameworks

LangGraph, CrewAI, Google ADK, OpenAI Agents and custom loops integrate through the plain data-plane API or the MemoryClient/MemorySessionManager helpers: write an event after each turn, retrieve records before assembling the prompt. The common pattern is a pre-model hook (retrieve and inject) plus a post-turn hook (persist).

16.3 AgentCore Harness (managed memory)

Harness — GA June 2026 — is the managed agent loop. It provisions and wires Memory for you, so the integration reduces to passing an actorId.

ModeBehaviour
Managed (default)Harness provisions a memory with semantic + summarization strategies and 30-day event expiry. Zero configuration. Cascade-deleted with the harness unless deleteManagedMemory=false.
Bring your ownAttach an existing memory ARN — for custom namespaces, KMS keys, or sharing one memory across several harnesses.
DisabledNo memory, no memory charges.
bash
# Managed memory with chosen strategies
aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-harness \
  --harness-name "MyHarness" \
  --execution-role-arn "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/MyHarnessRole" \
  --memory '{"managedMemoryConfiguration": {
      "strategies": ["SEMANTIC","SUMMARIZATION","USER_PREFERENCE"],
      "eventExpiryDuration": 60}}'

# Attach an existing memory instead
aws bedrock-agentcore-control update-harness \
  --harness-id "MyHarness-UuFdkQoXSL" \
  --memory '{"optionalValue": {"agentCoreMemoryConfiguration": {
      "arn": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:memory/MyMemory-abc123"}}}'

# Context-window handling when history outgrows the model
aws bedrock-agentcore-control update-harness \
  --harness-id "MyHarness-UuFdkQoXSL" \
  --truncation '{"strategy": "sliding_window",
                 "slidingWindowConfig": {"numMessages": 30}}'

Harness loads conversation history before reasoning and auto-injects relevant long-term records (defaults topK=10, relevanceScore=0.2). Truncation strategies are sliding_window (default), summarization, or none. Per-user scoping is a single parameter:

python
client.invoke_harness(
    harnessArn=HARNESS_ARN,
    runtimeSessionId=SESSION_ID,
    actorId="user-123",                       # isolates memory per user
    messages=[{"role": "user",
               "content": [{"text": "What do you remember about my preferences?"}]}],
)

17. IAM & permissions #

17.1 Caller policy

json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "MemoryDataPlane",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "bedrock-agentcore:CreateEvent",
        "bedrock-agentcore:ListEvents",
        "bedrock-agentcore:GetEvent",
        "bedrock-agentcore:RetrieveMemoryRecords",
        "bedrock-agentcore:ListMemoryRecords",
        "bedrock-agentcore:GetMemoryRecord"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:memory/CustomerSupportMemory-*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "ScopeRetrievalToTenantNamespace",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:RetrieveMemoryRecords"],
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringNotLike": { "bedrock-agentcore:namespace": "tenant-a/*" }
      }
    }
  ]
}

17.2 Memory execution role

Required when Memory itself must call other services — overrides (Bedrock), self-managed strategies (SNS/S3) and streaming (Kinesis).

json
// Trust policy
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRole",
    "Condition": {
      "ArnLike": { "aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:memory/*" }
    }
  }]
}
json
// Permissions policy
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "ModelInvocationForOverrides",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel", "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"],
      "Resource": ["arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/*",
                   "arn:aws:bedrock:*:*:inference-profile/*"],
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": { "aws:ResourceAccount": "${aws:PrincipalAccount}" }
      }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "RecordStreaming",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["kinesis:PutRecords", "kinesis:DescribeStream"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:kinesis:us-west-2:123456789012:stream/memory-records"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "SelfManagedDelivery",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["sns:Publish", "s3:PutObject"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:sns:us-west-2:123456789012:agentcore-memory-jobs",
        "arn:aws:s3:::my-agentcore-payloads/*"
      ]
    }
  ]
}

Also available: resource-based policies on the memory resource (Mar 2026) for cross-account and cross-team grants without touching caller roles, and bedrock-agentcore:namespace as a condition key for namespace-level authorisation.

18. Encryption #

All memory data — raw events and extracted records — is encrypted at rest by default with a service-owned KMS key. For regulated or sensitive workloads, supply a customer-managed key via encryptionKeyArn to gain key rotation control, revocation, and CloudTrail visibility into every decrypt.

json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "AllowAgentCoreMemoryKMS",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": [
      "kms:CreateGrant",
      "kms:Decrypt",
      "kms:DescribeKey",
      "kms:GenerateDataKey",
      "kms:GenerateDataKeyWithoutPlaintext",
      "kms:ReEncrypt*"
    ],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:kms:*:123456789012:key/*",
    "Condition": {
      "StringEquals": { "kms:ViaService": "bedrock-agentcore.us-east-1.amazonaws.com" }
    }
  }]
}

kms:CreateGrant is the one people forget — the service needs it to operate on your behalf asynchronously during extraction. Remember that revoking or scheduling deletion of the CMK renders existing memories unreadable; treat the key as part of the memory's lifecycle.

19. Observability #

19.1 Metrics

MetricMeasuresWatch for
LatencyEnd-to-end time from request to final response tokenRetrieval latency creeping into your turn budget
InvocationsData plane + control plane API calls and memory ingestion eventsCost driver — correlates directly with the per-event bill
Errors / System Errors / User ErrorsTotal, server-side, client-side failuresA rising user-error rate usually means quota or validation problems
ThrottlesThrottled invocations (also counted as invocations and user errors)The per-actor-per-session CreateEvent limit of 5 TPS is the usual culprit
Creation CountMemory events and memory records createdRecord growth rate — the long-term storage cost driver
StreamPublishingSuccess / Failure / StreamUserErrorKinesis record streaming healthUser errors indicate permissions or KMS key state issues

19.2 Logs

DestinationPath
Default log group/aws/vendedlogs/bedrock-agentcore/memory/APPLICATION_LOGS/{memory_id}
Custom log groupAny group whose name begins with /aws/vendedlogs/
OtherS3 and Firehose delivery destinations are also supported

Enable delivery with the CloudWatch Logs delivery APIs (PutDeliverySource → PutDeliveryDestination → CreateDelivery) against the memory ARN, the same pattern used for Gateway. In CloudWatch GenAI Observability, memory operations appear inside agent traces, so a slow or empty retrieval is visible in context rather than in isolation.

19.3 What to actually alarm on

  • Throttles on CreateEvent — the per-actor/session ceiling is low and chatty agents hit it.
  • Record creation rate trending up with flat user counts — extraction is duplicating rather than consolidating.
  • Retrieval returning zero results above your threshold — often a namespace-template mismatch, not an empty memory.
  • Extraction token-per-minute quota exhaustion, which silently delays long-term records.

20. Quotas & limits #

20.1 Resources

QuotaValueAdjustable
Memory resources per region per account150yes
Strategies per memory resource6no
Strategies per account900yes
Indexed keys per memory10no

20.2 Events

QuotaValueAdjustable
Minimum eventExpiryDuration7 daysno
Maximum eventExpiryDuration365 daysno
Messages per CreateEvent100no
Message size100 KBno
Event size10 MBno

20.3 API rates (per second, per account, per region)

APITPSAdjustable
CreateEvent200yes
CreateEvent per actor + session, conversational payloads5no
CreateEvent per actor + session, non-conversational10no
ListEvents200yes
DeleteEvent (per actor + session)20 (5)yes
RetrieveMemoryRecords30yes

20.4 Extraction

QuotaValueAdjustable
Tokens per minute for long-term extraction150,000yes
Tokens per minute for episodic extraction, per session50,000no
appendToPrompt size for custom strategies30 KBno
searchQuery length10,000 charsno
topK on retrievaldefault 10, max 100no
The limit you will actually hit

5 TPS of conversational CreateEvent per actor per session, and it is not adjustable. An agent that writes one event per message in a fast multi-agent exchange will throttle. Batch turns into a single event (up to 100 messages) rather than writing one event per message — this is exactly what the Strands batch_size parameter is for.

21. Pricing & cost model #

Verify before you budget

Rates below are published US on-demand rates as of 18 Aug 2026 and vary by region. Confirm against the AWS pricing page.

DimensionRateNotes
Short-term memory$0.25 per 1,000 new eventsCharged per CreateEvent, regardless of how many messages it contains
Long-term storage — built-in strategies$0.75 per 1,000 records / monthExtraction model cost is included
Long-term storage — built-in override or self-managed$0.25 per 1,000 records / monthCheaper storage, but model tokens are billed separately in your account
Long-term retrieval$0.50 per 1,000 retrievalsPer RetrieveMemoryRecords call

21.1 Worked example

10,000 monthly active users, 20 conversations each, 10 turns per conversation, batching 5 turns per event, 2 retrievals per turn, and roughly 50 long-term records accumulated per user:

text
Events    : 10,000 × 20 × (10 / 5)          =   400,000 events
            400,000 / 1,000 × $0.25                     = $100.00

Retrievals: 10,000 × 20 × 10 × 2            = 4,000,000 retrievals
            4,000,000 / 1,000 × $0.50                   = $2,000.00

Storage   : 10,000 × 50                     =   500,000 records
            500,000 / 1,000 × $0.75                     = $375.00
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Monthly total (excluding model tokens)                  ≈ $2,475

The shape of that number is the lesson: retrieval dominates. Two retrievals per turn is a natural design (preferences + facts) and it costs eight times what storing everything costs. Three levers, in order of impact:

  • Retrieve less often. Fetch preferences once per session, not once per turn, and cache them in the session's in-process state. In the example above that alone cuts the bill by roughly half.
  • Batch events. A single event carrying five turns costs one-fifth of five events — and simultaneously relieves the 5 TPS per-session ceiling.
  • Choose the right strategy tier. If you already operate model infrastructure, an override drops storage from $0.75 to $0.25 per 1,000 records — worth it above roughly a million records, less so below.

Also remember the second-order costs: overrides and self-managed strategies bill Bedrock tokens to your account, and self-managed pipelines add Lambda/SNS/S3/Kinesis charges.

22. Security & memory poisoning #

Memory introduces a threat class that stateless agents do not have: persistence of attacker-influenced content. A prompt injection against a stateless agent lasts one turn. The same injection against an agent with long-term memory can be extracted, consolidated into a record, and then retrieved into every future conversation — including other users' conversations if namespaces are sloppy.

Validate before you persist

Apply Bedrock Guardrails or your own filters to content on the way into memory, not only on the way out. Consolidation will faithfully preserve whatever it is given.

Namespaces are the isolation boundary

Always include {actorId} for anything personal, always use trailing slashes, and enforce with the bedrock-agentcore:namespace IAM condition key.

Treat retrieved records as untrusted input

A memory record is model-generated text that may reflect user-supplied content. Do not let it drive privileged actions without validation.

Scope reflections carefully

Cross-actor reflections generalise one user's data into insights served to others. Keep them impersonal or keep them actor-scoped.

Customer-managed KMS keys

For personal, health or payment data, use a CMK so you control rotation, revocation and audit.

Plan for erasure

A deletion request means events (DeleteEvent) and derived records (BatchDeleteMemoryRecords). Build the runbook before you need it — records outlive the events they came from.

Least privilege, per actor

The agent serving user A should not hold credentials that can retrieve user B's namespace. Scope at the IAM level, not only in application code.

Test adversarially

Include memory poisoning in your red-teaming: can a user get a false fact persisted about themselves, or about someone else?

Not a system of record

Long-term records are LLM inferences over conversation. They can be wrong, stale, or subtly distorted by consolidation. Never let a memory record be the authority for a balance, an entitlement, a medical fact or a legal status — retrieve those from the system that owns them and use memory only to know what to ask about.

23. Troubleshooting #

SymptomLikely causeFix
No long-term records ever appearNo strategies configured on the memoryAdd a strategy with UpdateMemory; without one, only short-term events are stored
Records appear minutes lateExtraction is asynchronous by designRead short-term events for recent context; only depend on records for cross-session recall
Retrieval returns nothingNamespace mismatch — querying /facts/user-1 when records are at /facts/user-1/, or the template resolved differentlyListMemoryRecords with namespacePath="/" to see where records actually landed, then fix the template
Cross-tenant records leak into resultsMissing trailing slash → prefix collision (/users/1 matches /users/10)Always terminate namespaces with /; add an IAM namespace condition as a backstop
ThrottledException on CreateEvent5 TPS per actor per session for conversational payloads — not adjustableBatch turns into one event (≤100 messages); raise the Strands batch_size
ServiceQuotaExceededExceptionMemory count, strategy count, or extraction tokens/minute exhaustedRequest an increase for the adjustable ones; reduce event volume for the rest
Records duplicate instead of consolidatingRecords are landing in different namespaces, so consolidation never compares themConsolidation operates within a namespace — put facts about one actor in one actor-scoped namespace
Extraction ignores tool outputSemantic and user-preference strategies process only USER/ASSISTANT rolesUse the episodic strategy for tool-aware extraction, and write TOOL payloads
Episodes never materialiseThe strategy emits an episode only once it detects completionEnsure conversations reach a resolution; include tool results so completion is detectable
New metadata filter returns nothingIndexed keys are not backfilled onto existing recordsExpect filters to apply only to records created after the key was added; re-import if needed
Metadata values inconsistent (High vs high)No validation.allowedValues on an LLM_INFERRED keyAdd allowed values, or switch to STRICTLY_CONSISTENT and set the value at write time
AccessDeniedException during extractionMissing memoryExecutionRoleArn or bedrock:InvokeModel on itAttach the execution role with model-invocation permissions; verify the trust policy
Streaming events never arriveExecution role lacks kinesis:PutRecords, or the stream's KMS key is unusableCheck StreamUserError; add kms:GenerateDataKey for encrypted streams
Self-managed strategy never firesTrigger thresholds never met (e.g. messageCount higher than a typical session)Add a timeBasedTrigger idle timeout as a floor
Old events won't expireeventExpiryDuration is applied per event at write timeUpdating the memory affects new events only; delete old ones explicitly
Unknown service from boto3Stale SDKpip install --upgrade boto3 botocore

23.1 Debug checklist

bash
# 1. Is the memory ACTIVE and are strategies attached?
aws bedrock-agentcore-control get-memory --memory-id "$MEMORY_ID"

# 2. Did the events actually land?
aws bedrock-agentcore list-events \
  --memory-id "$MEMORY_ID" --actor-id "customer-456" --session-id "session-789"

# 3. Where did records actually go? (widest possible net)
aws bedrock-agentcore list-memory-records \
  --memory-id "$MEMORY_ID" --namespace-path "/"

# 4. Does semantic search find them at that exact namespace?
aws bedrock-agentcore retrieve-memory-records \
  --memory-id "$MEMORY_ID" --namespace "/facts/customer-456/" \
  --search-criteria '{"searchQuery":"anything","topK":20}'

# 5. What do the service logs say?
aws logs tail "/aws/vendedlogs/bedrock-agentcore/memory/APPLICATION_LOGS/$MEMORY_ID" --follow

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway

A complete technical reference for the managed AI gateway that turns APIs, Lambda functions, MCP servers and model providers into governed, discoverable tools behind one secure endpoint — targets, protocols, dual authentication, semantic search, policy, interceptors, rate limits, quotas, pricing and troubleshooting.

Document revision: 18 August 2026  ·  Service status: Generally available (GA 13 Oct 2025)  ·  Control plane: bedrock-agentcore-control  ·  Endpoint: https://{gatewayId}.gateway.bedrock-agentcore.{region}.amazonaws.com
Fast-moving service

Gateway has changed shape substantially since GA — it began as an MCP tool server and is now a three-plane AI gateway. Configurable rate limits (Aug 2026), Web Search GA and Guardrails in Policy (Jun 2026), MCP sessions, streaming, elicitation and sampling (May 2026), HTTP passthrough targets, inference targets, Runtime targets and interceptors (Apr 2026), 3LO for MCP targets (Mar 2026). Every figure below reflects AWS documentation as of the revision date.

Architect's brief — read this first #

The parts that shape architecture rather than the parts you can look up. Everything here is expanded in the reference sections that follow.

0.1 The one idea everything else follows from

Gateway exists to kill the M×N problem: M agents each needing bespoke integration with N tools. It collapses that into M+N by becoming the single place where translation, authentication, discovery and governance happen.

It is not a proxy

A proxy forwards bytes. Gateway translates protocols (MCP ⇄ REST/Lambda/Smithy), exchanges credentials (your caller's identity for the target's credential), indexes tools for semantic discovery, and enforces policy. Those four jobs are why it can't simply be an ALB.

It has three distinct planes

MCP targets aggregate into one virtual MCP server. HTTP targets pass through untranslated. Inference targets route LLM traffic by model ID. One gateway can host all three, but they behave nothing alike — don't reason about them as one thing.

The mental model

Think API gateway for agents: same role Amazon API Gateway plays for HTTP clients — auth at the edge, per-route backends, throttling, observability — but speaking MCP, with credential exchange and semantic discovery bolted in because agents need to find tools, not just call them.

0.2 The decisions you cannot walk back

Semantic search is creation-time only

searchType is set in protocolConfiguration.mcpConfiguration at CreateGateway and cannot be added to an existing gateway. If you might ever exceed a few dozen tools, enable it on day one — otherwise you rebuild the gateway and repoint every client. → §14

Tool names are an API contract

Every tool is exposed as {targetName}___{toolName} — three underscores. That string is baked into agent prompts, Lambda routing logic and Cedar policy actions. Renaming a target silently breaks all three. Name targets as carefully as you'd name a public API. → §13

A gateway is a trust boundary

Inbound authorizer type, KMS key and service role are gateway-level. Every target behind it inherits that boundary. Mixing a public read-only API and a finance write API behind one gateway means one compromised token reaches both. Split by trust level, not by convenience. → §15

Two more that bite later

MCP server targets cache their capability catalogue. When the upstream server's tools change, the gateway does not notice — you must call SynchronizeGatewayTargets. And if you supplied a static mcpToolSchema upfront, you cannot synchronize at all without removing the schema first. → §9

DYNAMIC listing mode is incompatible with semantic search and 3LO. It looks attractive (capabilities discovered at runtime, always fresh) and quietly disqualifies you from the two features you most likely wanted.

0.3 What will surprise you in production

SurpriseWhy it happensWhat to do
Semantic search is rate-limited to 25 per minute, while ordinary tool calls get 200 TPS Search runs an embedding query, not a lookup. The default is roughly 0.4 TPS — three orders of magnitude below the tool-call limit. Search once per task to narrow the toolset, then call tools directly. Do not put search in a per-turn loop. Request an increase early if you need more. → §25
Search also costs 5× a tool call $0.025 per 1,000 search invocations vs. $0.005 per 1,000 tool/list/ping invocations, plus $0.02 per 100 tools indexed per month. Same fix as above, for the same reason. The economics and the quota point in one direction: search is for discovery, not dispatch. → §26
Your Lambda receives a prefixed tool name context.client_context.custom['bedrockAgentCoreToolName'] is myTarget___getOrder, not getOrder. Strip on the ___ delimiter before routing. Every Lambda target needs this — it is the single most common “why doesn't my tool fire” bug. → §6
Your OpenAPI spec probably won't import cleanly Swagger 2.0 is unsupported; oneOf/anyOf/allOf are unsupported; every operation needs an operationId because that becomes the tool name. Budget real time for spec remediation. Treat the spec as agent-facing product surface: operationId and description are what the model reads to decide whether to call it. → §7
Built-in integration templates are console-only Salesforce, Slack, Jira, ServiceNow and the rest cannot be added via API. They break infrastructure-as-code. For reproducible environments, model those targets as OpenAPI or MCP-server targets instead. → §10
Web Search costs $7 per 1,000 queries That is ~1,400× a normal gateway tool invocation. It is a fine feature and a real line item. Gate it behind policy or rate limits so an agent in a retry loop cannot spend four figures overnight. → §26

0.4 The thing that actually determines whether agents work

Gateway makes it trivial to expose a thousand tools. That is a trap. Every tool in tools/list consumes context window and adds a wrong turn the model can take; tool overload degrades accuracy long before it hits any quota. The whole reason x_amz_bedrock_agentcore_search exists is that listing everything does not scale.

Curate per agent

Prefer several small, purpose-scoped gateways over one giant catalogue. A support agent should not see the deployment tools.

Search, then call

Narrow to a handful of candidate tools at the start of a task, then invoke directly. Discovery once, dispatch many.

Descriptions are prompt engineering

The description on every tool and parameter is the model's instruction manual. Vague descriptions cause wrong tool selection far more often than bad model choice does.

0.5 Security posture in one page

  agent / client
      │  INBOUND: JWT (OAuth 2.0) | AWS_IAM (SigV4) | AUTHENTICATE_ONLY | NONE
      ▼
 ┌──────────────────────────── GATEWAY ────────────────────────────┐
 │  authorizer  →  REQUEST interceptor  →  policy engine (Cedar)   │
 │                                          default-deny in ENFORCE│
 │  rate limits (RPS/RPM · TPM · connections, scoped by claim)     │
 │  credential exchange via AgentCore Identity token vault         │
 │                        ↓                                        │
 │  RESPONSE interceptor  ←  target response                       │
 └────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │  OUTBOUND (per target, independent of inbound):
                          │  GATEWAY_IAM_ROLE · API_KEY · OAUTH 2LO/3LO/token-exchange
                          ▼
            Lambda · REST API · Smithy service · MCP server · model provider

Three properties are worth internalising:

  • Inbound and outbound are fully decoupled. A user authenticates to the gateway with a Cognito JWT; the gateway authenticates to Salesforce with an OAuth credential from the token vault. The user never holds the downstream credential. This is the single biggest security win over letting agents call APIs directly.
  • Policy is default-deny in ENFORCE mode, evaluated at the gateway rather than inside agent code — so a jailbroken prompt cannot talk its way past it. Cedar conditions can even inspect tool arguments (context.input.amount < 1000). → §17
  • Interceptors are a Lambda in the hot path. Powerful for custom authz and redaction, but they add latency to every call, must be idempotent (gateways retry), and passRequestHeaders: true hands your bearer tokens to that Lambda. Default it to false. → §18

0.6 When not to use it

One agent, three tools

Native function calling in your framework is simpler and free. Gateway earns its keep at fan-out, shared tooling, or when credentials must not live with the agent.

Ultra-low-latency calls

You are adding a managed hop plus optional interceptor Lambdas. For a millisecond-sensitive inner loop, call the service directly.

Streaming bulk data

6 MB payload cap on tool calls, no Smithy streaming operations. Return a pointer (S3 presigned URL), not the payload.

The clearest signal you should use it: more than one agent needs the same tool, or a tool's credential must not be handed to the agent. Both are org-shaped problems, and Gateway is an org-shaped answer.

1. Overview #

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway is a fully managed AI gateway: a single, secure entry point through which agents reach tools, other agents and language models. It converts existing enterprise assets — REST APIs, Lambda functions, AWS service models, MCP servers — into agent-ready tools without writing an MCP server, and governs every call that flows through.

1.1 The six jobs it does

Security guard

OAuth/JWT or SigV4 authorization at the edge; validates that the caller is a legitimate user or agent.

Translation

Converts MCP requests into REST calls, Lambda invocations and Smithy operations, and formats responses back.

Composition

Aggregates many APIs, functions, MCP servers, agents and model providers behind one endpoint.

Credential exchange

Injects the right credential per target from the AgentCore Identity token vault — the agent never sees it.

Semantic tool selection

Natural-language discovery across hundreds or thousands of tools via a built-in search tool.

Infrastructure manager

Serverless, autoscaling, with CloudWatch metrics, vended logs and CloudTrail auditing built in.
