Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity

A complete technical reference for the identity and credential-management service for AI agents — workload identities, the token vault, inbound JWT authorization, outbound OAuth and API-key flows, on-behalf-of exchange, private IdPs, IAM scoping, quotas, pricing and troubleshooting.

Document revision: 18 August 2026  ·  Service status: Generally available (GA 13 Oct 2025)  ·  Control plane: bedrock-agentcore-control  ·  Data plane: bedrock-agentcore
Fast-moving service

Identity has gained substantial capability since GA — private key JWT client authentication backed by KMS (Jul 2026), external Secrets Manager references (Jun 2026), VPC egress to private IdPs, on-behalf-of token exchange and two new regions (Apr 2026), resource-based policies and control-plane PrivateLink (Mar 2026), VPC condition keys (Jan 2026). Figures below reflect AWS documentation as of the revision date.

Architect's brief — read this first #

The parts that shape architecture rather than the parts you can look up. Everything here is expanded in the reference sections that follow.

0.1 The one idea everything else follows from

Identity exists to solve the confused deputy problem for agents. An agent is a piece of software acting on behalf of a human. If it holds a credential, that credential is usually far more powerful than the human it is serving — and the agent is non-deterministic, so you cannot reason about when it will use it.

AgentCore Identity's answer is a double binding: every credential in the vault is bound to a pair — the workload identity (which agent) and the end-user identity (on whose behalf).

            credential in the token vault
                        │
        ┌───────────────┴────────────────┐
        │                                │
  workload identity                end-user identity
  "which agent is asking"          "on whose behalf"
        │                                │
        └───────────────┬────────────────┘
                        ▼
         a token that is useless to any other
         agent, and useless for any other user
Why that pairing is the whole product

It means agent A cannot retrieve user B's Google token even with valid IAM permission to call the API. Authorization to call the credential provider is IAM; authorization to receive a specific user's credential comes from the workload access token. Two independent gates — which is exactly what you want protecting a token that can read someone's mailbox.

0.2 The decisions you cannot walk back

The user ID namespace is the vault key

Credentials are filed under the end-user identity. If you ever federate a second IdP, user123 from Cognito and user123 from Auth0 collide into one vault entry. Partition from day one — cognito+user123, auth0+user123. Retrofitting means every user re-consents. → §7

Provider names are code

@requires_access_token(provider_name="google-provider") hard-codes a name into every agent. Renaming a provider means redeploying every agent that references it, and existing vault entries do not follow. Name them for the service and environment, not the ticket. → §10

Callback URLs are registered with the IdP

Creating a provider returns a callbackUrl you must add to the OAuth app's redirect list, and 3LO return URLs must be pre-registered on the workload identity. Both are external-system changes with their own change-control. Plan the round trip. → §15

The one to get right before you ship

GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId accepts a user identifier as an opaque, unverified string. Whoever can call it can impersonate any user and receive that user's stored credentials. It exists for development and for architectures where a trusted upstream already authenticated the caller. In production, grant GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT and explicitly Deny the ForUserId variant. Note that the managed policy BedrockAgentCoreFullAccess includes it. → §7

0.3 What will surprise you in production

SurpriseWhy it happensWhat to do
3LO requires you to build and host a callback endpoint The consent flow redirects a browser back to your HTTPS endpoint, which must validate the session and call CompleteResourceTokenAuth. Even agents on Runtime must host this themselves. Budget for a real web endpoint with session validation. This is the single most underestimated piece of an Identity integration. → §15
Authorization URLs expire in 10 minutes Both the auth URL and its session URI are short-lived and bound to the initiating user. Don't queue consent for later or email the link. Present it in-session, and design the agent to pause and resume cleanly.
Only 50 credential providers per type, per account 50 OAuth2, 50 API key, 50 payment — adjustable, but low if each team or environment wants its own. Plan the naming and sharing model early; request an increase before you hit it, not after. → §21
It's free through Runtime and Gateway, $0.010 per 1,000 requests standalone Pricing follows how you consume it, not what you use. Rarely material either way — but it removes any cost argument against using Identity properly rather than stuffing secrets into env vars. → §22
The first call for each user pays a consent round trip Tokens are cached in the vault per user+workload; the first 3LO call needs a human. Warm consent during onboarding rather than mid-task, or use OBO exchange to skip consent entirely where your IdP supports it. → §16
Runtime-managed identities can't fetch their own tokens directly When Runtime or Gateway handles inbound auth, it injects the workload access token for you and blocks direct retrieval. Read it from the invocation context rather than calling the API. Only self-hosted agents call GetWorkloadAccessToken* themselves. → §7

0.4 The design problem nobody warns you about: consent

Two-legged flows are easy — the agent uses its own credential, no human involved. Three-legged flows put a human in the middle of an autonomous process, and that breaks the usual agent programming model. An agent that hits a tool needing 3LO must stop, surface a URL, wait for a browser round trip, and resume — possibly minutes later, possibly never.

Consent up front

Collect authorizations during onboarding for the services an agent will need, so runtime is always a cache hit. Best UX, requires knowing the toolset in advance.

Design for the pause

Treat "needs consent" as a first-class agent outcome with its own resumable state — not an error. Long-running Runtime sessions make this tractable.

Skip it with OBO

If the downstream resource trusts your IdP, on-behalf-of token exchange converts the inbound user token into a downstream token with no consent flow at all.

The third option is strictly better when available, which makes "does my IdP support RFC 8693 token exchange or RFC 7523 JWT grants?" one of the highest-leverage questions to ask early.

0.5 Security posture in one page

  user ──JWT──► agent (Runtime / Gateway / self-hosted)
                   │
                   │ 1. inbound: validate iss / aud / client_id / scope
                   ▼
        GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT      ← cryptographic proof of user identity
                   │                        (…ForUserId = unverified. deny in prod)
                   ▼
        workload access token  =  { agent identity + user identity }
                   │
                   │ 2. IAM gate: may this role call this credential provider?
                   ▼
        ┌──────────────── TOKEN VAULT ────────────────┐
        │  oauth2-credential-provider/google-provider │
        │  api-key/stripe-provider                    │
        │  payment/coinbase-provider                  │
        │  secrets MANAGED or EXTERNAL (Secrets Mgr)  │
        └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘
                               │ 3. vault gate: is there a credential stored
                               │    for THIS user under THIS workload?
                               ▼
             M2M (2LO)  ·  USER_FEDERATION (3LO)  ·  ON_BEHALF_OF_TOKEN_EXCHANGE
                               │
                               ▼
                      downstream API called with a
                      short-lived, user-scoped token

Three properties worth internalising:

  • The agent never holds a long-lived secret. Client secrets and API keys live in the vault (or your own Secrets Manager secret); the agent receives only short-lived access tokens, and only for the user it is serving.
  • Two independent gates. IAM decides which providers a role may ask for; the workload access token decides whose credentials come back. Compromising one does not get you the data.
  • Private key JWT removes the shared secret entirely. Since July 2026 you can authenticate to an IdP with a KMS-held signing key — the private key never leaves KMS and every signature is in CloudTrail. For high-value providers this is meaningfully stronger than a client secret. → §10.4

0.6 When not to use it

Only AWS resources

If your agent just calls Bedrock, S3 and DynamoDB, the execution role is the whole answer. Identity adds nothing.

One shared service account

A single machine credential with no per-user scoping is an API-key provider at best — and honestly, Secrets Manager alone may suffice.

No user delegation

The value is concentrated in "act as this specific human, against that third party." Without that, you are paying complexity for little return.

The clearest signal you should use it: an agent needs to touch a user's own data in a third-party system — their Drive, their Jira, their Salesforce records. That is precisely the case where handing the agent a credential is unacceptable and the double binding earns its keep.

1. Overview #

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity is a purpose-built identity and credential-management service for AI agents and automated workloads. It gives every agent a durable identity, validates who is calling that agent, and brokers the credentials the agent needs to act on a user's behalf against AWS and third-party services — without the agent ever holding a long-lived secret.

11,000
Workload identities
25
Built-in OAuth vendors
3
Outbound flow types
50
Providers per type
200 TPS
Token retrieval rate
Free
Via Runtime / Gateway

1.1 What it actually does

Gives agents an identity

Workload identities are a stable anchor that survives across deployment environments and authentication schemes — IAM roles, OAuth tokens, API keys — and are the subject of every credential decision.

Validates inbound callers

OAuth 2.0 / OIDC JWT validation against your IdP, with issuer, audience, client and scope checks, plus custom claims and workload-source enforcement.

Stores and brokers credentials

A managed token vault holding OAuth client credentials, API keys and payment credentials — with secrets either service-managed or referenced from your own Secrets Manager secrets.

Runs the OAuth dance

2LO client credentials, 3LO authorization code with consent and session binding, and RFC-standard on-behalf-of token exchange — all handled by the service.

Scopes access two ways

IAM controls which providers a role may call; the workload access token controls whose credentials are returned. Both must pass.

Audits everything

CloudWatch metrics per workload identity, provider and flow type; CloudTrail for management events and KMS signing operations.

2. Where Identity fits in AgentCore #

ServiceResponsibilityRelationship to Identity
IdentityAgent identity, inbound authorization, outbound credential brokering—
RuntimeServerless, session-isolated agent hostingCreates a workload identity automatically, validates inbound JWTs, and injects the workload access token into the agent
GatewayUnified tool/agent/model endpointAlso gets a workload identity automatically; every outbound target credential comes from the Identity token vault
MemoryShort/long-term agent memoryIdentity supplies the verified user identity that should drive actorId
PolicyCedar authorization for tool callsComplementary: Identity establishes who, Policy decides what they may do
ObservabilityCloudWatch GenAI traces and metricsIdentity emits token-fetch success/failure/throttle metrics by provider and flow type
Usable standalone

Identity is a plain AWS API. An agent on ECS, EKS, Lambda or a laptop can create a workload identity manually and call the same token APIs — AWS publishes a reference architecture for exactly this on Amazon ECS. Runtime and Gateway simply automate the wiring (and make it free).

3. When to use it #

DimensionAgentCore IdentitySecrets Manager aloneRoll your own OAuth brokerIAM only
Per-user credential scopingBuilt in (double binding)NoneYou build itn/a
3LO consent flowManaged, with session bindingNoneYou build and secure itn/a
Token refresh & cachingManaged in the vaultManualYou build itn/a
Third-party services25 built-in vendors + customStore the secret onlyAnything you implementNo
AWS resourcesVia the execution rolen/an/aNative and sufficient
Agent holds a long-lived secret?NoYes, effectivelyDependsNo
Ops burdenVery lowLowHigh, and security-criticalNone

3.1 Good fits

  • Agents acting on a user's own data in third-party SaaS — Google Workspace, Microsoft 365, Slack, Salesforce, Atlassian, GitHub.
  • Multi-tenant agents where per-user credential isolation is a hard requirement.
  • Enterprises with an existing IdP (Okta, Entra ID, Auth0, PingOne, OneLogin, CyberArk) that agents must federate with.
  • Any design where handing the agent a long-lived secret would fail a security review.
  • Agents needing on-behalf-of access across internal services without repeated consent.

3.2 Poor fits

  • Agents that only touch AWS resources — the execution role already does this.
  • Pure machine-to-machine integrations with one shared credential and no user dimension.
  • Environments where you cannot register callback URLs with the third-party OAuth app.

4. Core concepts #

ConceptDefinitionNotes
Workload identityThe digital identity of an agent within AWS. A stable anchor across environments and auth schemes.Auto-created by Runtime and Gateway; created manually for self-hosted agents.
Workload identity directoryThe container for workload identities — default in most accounts.ARN: …:workload-identity-directory/default/workload-identity/NAME
Workload access tokenA token carrying both the agent identity and the end-user identity. The key that unlocks the vault.Injected automatically by Runtime/Gateway; fetched via SDK otherwise.
Token vaultManaged encrypted store for OAuth client credentials, API keys, payment credentials and cached user tokens.ARN: …:token-vault/default/.... default vault per account.
Credential providerConfiguration describing how to obtain a credential for one downstream resource.Three kinds: OAuth2, API key, payment. 50 of each per account.
Resource tokenThe short-lived downstream credential returned to the agent (OAuth access token or API key).Scoped to the user in the workload access token.
Inbound authValidating who may invoke the agent or gateway.SigV4 or OIDC JWT.
Outbound authObtaining credentials for what the agent calls next.M2M, USER_FEDERATION, or ON_BEHALF_OF_TOKEN_EXCHANGE.

5. Inbound vs outbound #

Inbound authOutbound auth
Question answeredWho is calling my agent, and may they?What credential may my agent use next, and for whom?
MechanismIAM SigV4, or OIDC JWT validation against your IdPToken vault lookup keyed by workload identity + user identity
Configured onThe Runtime or Gateway resource (authorizerConfiguration)Credential providers, referenced by name from agent code or gateway targets
ProducesA workload access tokenA short-lived resource token (OAuth access token or API key)
Failure looks like401 with WWW-Authenticate, or 403AccessDeniedException, or an authorization URL requiring consent
They are independent by design

A user can authenticate to your agent with a Cognito JWT while the agent calls Salesforce with a vault-held OAuth credential. Nothing about the inbound scheme constrains the outbound one. That decoupling is what keeps downstream credentials out of the user's session and out of the agent's code.

6. Inbound authentication #

6.1 JWT authorizer configuration

python
authorizerConfiguration = {
    "customJWTAuthorizer": {
        "discoveryUrl": "https://cognito-idp.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/us-east-1_example/.well-known/openid-configuration",
        "allowedClients": ["your-client-id"],
        "allowedAudience": ["your-audience"],
        "allowedScopes": ["aws.cognito.signin.user.admin"],
    }
}
Authorizer fieldJWT claimValidation
discoveryUrl → issuerissMust match the issuer published at the OIDC discovery endpoint
allowedClientsclient_idMust appear in the allowed list
allowedAudienceaudAt least one audience value must match
allowedScopesscopeToken scopes must include the authorized scopes

6.2 Restricting the caller to a gateway

Since April 2026, allowedWorkloadConfiguration pins which hosting environments may invoke a runtime — closing the bypass where someone with the runtime URL skips the gateway and its policy engine.

json
{
  "customJWTAuthorizer": {
    "discoveryUrl": "https://cognito-idp.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/us-east-1_example/.well-known/openid-configuration",
    "allowedClients": ["your-client-id"],
    "allowedWorkloadConfiguration": {
      "hostingEnvironments": [
        { "arn": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:111122223333:gateway/my-gateway-id" }
      ]
    }
  }
}

6.3 Reading user claims inside the agent

python
import jwt, json

@app.entrypoint
def invoke(payload, context):
    auth_header = context.request_headers.get("Authorization")
    if auth_header:
        token = auth_header.replace("Bearer ", "")
        # Signature already verified by AgentCore — decode for claims only
        claims = jwt.decode(token, options={"verify_signature": False})
        app.logger.info("User claims: %s", json.dumps(claims))

6.4 Debugging a rejected token

bash
echo "$TOKEN" | cut -d '.' -f2 | tr '_-' '/+' \
  | awk '{ l=4 - length($0)%4; if (l<4) printf "%s", $0; for (i=0; i<l; i++) printf "="; print "" }' \
  | base64 -D | jq

Check four things in order: iss matches the discovery document's issuer; client_id is in allowedClients; aud is in allowedAudience; and exp hasn't passed.

Unauthenticated response

Calls without a bearer token return RFC 7235 discovery metadata, so a compliant MCP or OAuth client can find the authorization server automatically:

WWW-Authenticate: Bearer resource_metadata="https://bedrock-agentcore.REGION.amazonaws.com/runtimes/ESCAPED_ARN/invocations/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource?qualifier=QUALIFIER"

7. Workload access tokens #

The workload access token is the credential that unlocks the vault. It carries the agent's workload identity and the end-user's identity, and everything downstream is scoped by that pair.

PatternUser verificationUse whenRisk
Automatic (Runtime / Gateway)Cryptographic, done for youStandard hosted deployments with inbound authlowest
GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWTCryptographic — validates signature, issuer and expiry, uses iss + subSelf-hosted agents holding a real IdP JWTlow
GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserIdNone — the user ID is an opaque stringDevelopment, or a trusted upstream that already authenticatedhighest

7.1 The automatic path

  1. Runtime validates the inbound OAuth token against the authorizer configuration.
  2. It extracts the iss and sub claims as the verified end-user identity.
  3. It resolves the agent's workload identity.
  4. It calls GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT internally.
  5. The resulting token is delivered to the agent via the WorkloadAccessToken header.

Runtime-managed identities cannot retrieve these tokens directly — read the injected value instead of calling the API.

7.2 The self-hosted path

python
from bedrock_agentcore.services.identity import IdentityClient

identity_client = IdentityClient("us-east-1")

# Preferred: verified end-user identity from a real IdP token
token = identity_client.get_workload_access_token(
    workload_name="my-demo-agent",
    user_token=INBOUND_JWT,
)

# Development only: unverified opaque identifier
token = identity_client.get_workload_access_token(
    workload_name="my-demo-agent",
    user_id="cognito+user123",          # note the IdP prefix — see below
)
Two rules for ForUserId

Derive the identifier from an authenticated principal — the IAM caller, a validated session attribute — never from request input. And partition per IdP: cognito+user123 vs auth0+user123. Without the prefix, two different humans with the same subject in two directories share one vault entry, which is both a data leak and an unrecoverable migration.

json
{
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "DenyUnverifiedUserIdentity",
    "Effect": "Deny",
    "Action": "bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId",
    "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default"
  }]
}

8. Workload identities #

DeploymentCreationNotes
AgentCore RuntimeAutomaticARN returned in the CreateAgentRuntime response as workloadIdentityDetails
AgentCore GatewayAutomaticSame, on CreateGateway
Self-hosted / hybrid / testingManual via CreateWorkloadIdentityLets you control the name and metadata, and run several identities for one agent across environments
bash
aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-workload-identity \
  --name my-agent-identity

# Register 3LO return URLs (required before any user-federation flow)
aws bedrock-agentcore-control update-workload-identity \
  --name GoogleCalendarAgent \
  --allowed-resource-oauth2-return-urls https://myagentapp.example.com/callback

aws bedrock-agentcore-control list-workload-identities
Identity granularity is your access-control granularity

IAM policies scope credential-provider access by workload identity. One identity shared by every agent means every agent can reach every provider. Give each agent — or each trust tier — its own workload identity and its own role, and the scope-down policies in §19 become genuinely meaningful rather than decorative.

9. Token vault #

The token vault is the managed, encrypted store behind everything: OAuth client credentials, API keys, payment credentials, and the per-user access and refresh tokens obtained through consent flows.

text
arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:REGION:ACCOUNT:token-vault/default
arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:REGION:ACCOUNT:token-vault/default/oauth2-credential-provider/PROVIDER_NAME
arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:REGION:ACCOUNT:token-vault/default/api-key/PROVIDER_NAME

9.1 Secret sources

clientSecretSourceBehaviourUse when
MANAGEDAgentCore creates and manages the underlying secret. Simplest path.Getting started; secrets with no special governance needs
EXTERNALReferences an existing AWS Secrets Manager secret by ARN and JSON key (added June 2026).You need your own CMK, tagging, rotation schedule or resource policy on the secret
json
{
  "githubOauth2ProviderConfig": {
    "clientId": "your-github-client-id",
    "clientSecretSource": "EXTERNAL",
    "clientSecretConfig": {
      "secretId": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789012:secret:my-secret",
      "jsonKey": "clientSecret"
    }
  }
}

Caching. User tokens obtained through 3LO are cached in the vault against the user + workload pair, so subsequent calls are immediate. Only the first call for a given user pays the consent round trip — which is exactly why warming consent during onboarding is worth the effort.

10. OAuth2 credential providers #

10.1 Supported vendors

GoogleOauth2GithubOauth2SlackOauth2 SalesforceOauth2MicrosoftOauth2AtlassianOauth2 LinkedinOauth2XOauth2FacebookOauth2 ZoomOauth2TwitchOauth2SpotifyOauth2 DropboxOauth2NotionOauth2HubspotOauth2 RedditOauth2YandexOauth2 OktaOauth2Auth0Oauth2CognitoOauth2 OneLoginOauth2PingOneOauth2CyberArkOauth2 FusionAuthOauth2CustomOauth2

10.2 Creating a provider

bash
# AgentCore CLI
agentcore add credential --type oauth --name github-provider \
  --discovery-url https://your-idp/.well-known/openid-configuration \
  --client-id your-client-id --client-secret your-client-secret \
  --scopes repo,user
agentcore deploy

# AWS CLI — capture the callback URL, you will need it
RESP=$(aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-oauth2-credential-provider \
  --name "google-provider" \
  --credential-provider-vendor "GoogleOauth2" \
  --oauth2-provider-config-input '{
      "googleOauth2ProviderConfig": {
        "clientId": "your-client-id",
        "clientSecret": "your-client-secret"
      }}' --output json)

echo "$RESP" | jq -r '.callbackUrl'   # → register this in the OAuth app
python
from bedrock_agentcore.services.identity import IdentityClient

identity_client = IdentityClient("us-east-1")
provider = identity_client.create_oauth2_credential_provider({
    "name": "github-provider",
    "credentialProviderVendor": "GithubOauth2",
    "oauth2ProviderConfigInput": {
        "githubOauth2ProviderConfig": {
            "clientId": "your-github-client-id",
            "clientSecret": "your-github-client-secret",
        }
    },
})
Don't lose the callback URL

The callbackUrl is returned once at creation and must be added to your OAuth application's redirect URI allow-list. Miss this and every 3LO flow fails at the provider with a redirect-mismatch error that says nothing about AgentCore.

10.3 Custom providers

json
{
  "customOauth2ProviderConfig": {
    "oauthDiscovery": {
      "discoveryUrl": "https://my.idp.example.com/.well-known/openid-configuration"
      // …or supply authorizationServerMetadata explicitly:
      // "authorizationServerMetadata": {
      //   "issuer": "...", "authorizationEndpoint": "...", "tokenEndpoint": "...",
      //   "responseTypes": ["code"], "tokenEndpointAuthMethods": ["client_secret_basic"] }
    },
    "clientId": "your-client-id",
    "clientSecret": "your-client-secret",
    "clientAuthenticationMethod": "CLIENT_SECRET_BASIC"
  }
}

clientAuthenticationMethod accepts CLIENT_SECRET_BASIC, CLIENT_SECRET_POST, AWS_IAM_ID_TOKEN_JWT and PRIVATE_KEY_JWT.

10.4 Private key JWT (no shared secret)

Added July 2026. The agent authenticates to the IdP with a signed JWT rather than a client secret; the private key lives in AWS KMS and never leaves it, and every signing operation lands in CloudTrail. Works across M2M, OBO and user-delegated flows.

json
{
  "clientAuthenticationMethod": "PRIVATE_KEY_JWT",
  "privateKeyJwtConfig": {
    "privateKeySource": { "kmsKeySource": { "kmsKeyArn": "arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:123456789012:key/abcd-1234" } },
    "signingAlgorithm": "RS256",
    "additionalHeaderClaims":  { "kid": "my-key-id" },
    "additionalPayloadClaims": { "custom": "value" }
  }
}

Signing algorithms: RS256, PS256, ES256.

Worth the extra setup for high-value providers

A client secret is a bearer value — anyone who reads it anywhere can use it. A KMS-backed signing key cannot be exfiltrated at all, and each use is independently auditable. For the provider that reaches your CRM or your finance system, that difference is the whole point.

11. API key credential providers #

bash
agentcore add credential --name your-service-name --api-key your-api-key

aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-api-key-credential-provider \
  --name "your-service-name" \
  --api-key-secret-source EXTERNAL \
  --api-key-secret-config secretId=arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789012:secret:my-secret,jsonKey=api_key
python
import asyncio
from bedrock_agentcore.identity.auth import requires_api_key

@requires_api_key(provider_name="your-service-name")
async def call_service(*, api_key: str):
    # api_key is injected; never stored in the agent
    ...

asyncio.run(call_service())
API keys have no user dimension

Unlike OAuth, an API key is a single shared credential — the double binding degrades to workload-identity scoping only. That is still a real improvement over an environment variable (rotation, audit, IAM scoping), but do not mistake it for per-user isolation. Where the downstream service supports OAuth, prefer it.

12. Payment credential providers #

A distinct provider type for agentic payment workflows. Secrets live in AWS Secrets Manager and only ARNs are surfaced to agents. Retrieved via GetResourcePaymentToken.

VendorConfiguration keyFields
CoinbaseCDPcoinbaseCdpConfigurationapiKeyId, apiKeySecret, walletSecret (or *SecretSource: EXTERNAL variants)
StripePrivystripePrivyConfigurationappId, appSecret, authorizationPrivateKey, authorizationId
bash
aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-payment-credential-provider \
  --name "coinbase-provider" \
  --credential-provider-vendor "CoinbaseCDP" \
  --provider-configuration-input '{
    "coinbaseCdpConfiguration": {
      "apiKeyId": "your-coinbase-api-key-id",
      "apiKeySecretSource": "EXTERNAL",
      "apiKeySecretConfig": {
        "secretId": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789012:secret:my-api-secret",
        "jsonKey": "apiKeySecret" },
      "walletSecretSource": "EXTERNAL",
      "walletSecretConfig": {
        "secretId": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789012:secret:my-wallet-secret",
        "jsonKey": "walletSecret" }
    }}'
Treat these as a separate trust tier

A payment credential is the one an autonomous agent can turn into irreversible loss. Give it its own workload identity, an explicit IAM allow-list, a Cedar policy bounding transaction amounts at the Gateway, and human approval for anything above a threshold. Do not let it share a role with general-purpose tools.

13. M2M — two-legged OAuth #

The agent authenticates with its own client credentials. No user, no consent, no browser.

python
from bedrock_agentcore.identity.auth import requires_access_token

@requires_access_token(
    provider_name="system-provider",
    scopes=["service-account-scope"],
    auth_flow="M2M",
)
async def machine_to_machine(*, access_token: str):
    ...

Use it for backend integrations, system services and any downstream that authorizes the application rather than the user. It is the simplest flow and the one to prefer whenever per-user scoping is not genuinely required — every 3LO you avoid is a consent flow you don't have to design.

14. USER_FEDERATION — three-legged OAuth #

The agent obtains a token scoped to a specific end user, with that user's explicit consent.

  agent code calls @requires_access_token(auth_flow="USER_FEDERATION")
        │
        ▼
  workload access token ──► GetResourceOauth2Token
        │
        ├── cached token for this user+workload?  ──yes──►  return it (fast path)
        │
        no
        ▼
  AgentCore returns an authorizationUrl + sessionUri (both valid 10 minutes)
        │
        ▼
  on_auth_url callback: show the URL to the user
        │
        ▼
  user authenticates with the provider and grants consent
        │
        ▼
  provider redirects to YOUR registered callback endpoint (?session_id=…)
        │
        ▼
  your endpoint validates the session, then calls CompleteResourceTokenAuth
        │
        ▼
  token cached in the vault, bound to user + workload
        │
        ▼
  agent receives access_token and calls the downstream API
python
import asyncio
from bedrock_agentcore.identity.auth import requires_access_token

@requires_access_token(
    provider_name="google-provider",
    scopes=["https://www.googleapis.com/auth/drive.metadata.readonly"],
    auth_flow="USER_FEDERATION",
    on_auth_url=lambda url: print("Authorize here:", url),
    force_authentication=True,
    callback_url="https://myagentapp.example.com/callback",
)
async def read_from_google_drive(*, access_token: str):
    import requests
    r = requests.get("https://www.googleapis.com/drive/v3/files",
                     headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {access_token}"})
    return r.json()

asyncio.run(read_from_google_drive())

14.1 GetResourceOauth2Token parameters

ParameterReq.Notes
workloadIdentityToken✔The workload access token (agent + user identity)
resourceCredentialProviderName✔1–128 chars, [a-zA-Z0-9\-_]+
scopes✔OAuth scopes requested
oauth2Flow✔USER_FEDERATION | M2M | ON_BEHALF_OF_TOKEN_EXCHANGE
resourceOauth2ReturnUrl—Callback URL; must be pre-registered on the workload identity
forceAuthentication—Always start a fresh 3LO flow rather than using a cached token
customState—Opaque CSRF value echoed to your callback (up to 4,096 chars)
customParameters, audiences, resources—Extra authorization-request parameters and target resources
sessionUri—urn:ietf:params:oauth:request_uri:… — identifies an in-flight session

Response: accessToken, authorizationUrl, sessionStatus (IN_PROGRESS | FAILED) and sessionUri.

15. Authorization URL session binding #

The attack it prevents

User A generates an authorization URL and it reaches User B — pasted in a chat, logged, forwarded. Without binding, User B completes the flow and the resulting token is stored against User A's vault entry, giving B access to A's resources. Session binding verifies that whoever initiated the request is whoever granted consent.

15.1 The four steps

  1. The agent calls GetResourceOauth2Token, which returns an authorization URL and a session URI.
  2. The user authorizes; AgentCore Identity redirects to your HTTPS callback with ?session_id=….
  3. Your application verifies that the currently logged-in user matches the original requester — from the active session, not a remote cache.
  4. Your application calls CompleteResourceTokenAuth with the session URI and user identifier; the token is fetched and stored.
bash
aws bedrock-agentcore-control update-workload-identity \
  --name GoogleCalendarAgent \
  --allowed-resource-oauth2-return-urls https://myagentapp.example.com/callback
python
def handle_3lo_callback(request):
    session_id = request.query_params.get("session_id")
    if not session_id:
        return JSONResponse(status_code=400, content={"message": "missing session_id"})

    # Identity MUST come from the live application session, not a lookup keyed by session_id
    session_details = validate_session_cookies(request.cookies.get("my-application-cookie"))
    user_id = session_details.get(USER_ID)
    if not user_id:
        return JSONResponse(status_code=500, content={"message": "Internal Server Error"})

    IdentityClient(REGION).complete_resource_token_auth(
        session_uri=session_id,
        user_identifier=UserIdIdentifier(user_id=user_id),
    )
    return JSONResponse(status_code=200, content={"message": "OAuth2 3LO flow completed"})

15.2 Non-negotiables

  • Pre-register every return URL on the workload identity via allowedResourceOauth2ReturnUrls. Missing this produces the “you must register and use at least one ResourceOauth2ReturnUrl” error.
  • HTTPS only for the callback endpoint.
  • Resolve the user from the live session — cookies or local storage — never from a cache keyed by the session ID, which would defeat the binding entirely.
  • Pass an opaque customState for CSRF protection and verify it on return.
  • 10-minute window for both the URL and the session URI.
  • You host the endpoint, even when the agent runs on AgentCore Runtime.

16. On-behalf-of token exchange #

Added April 2026, and the most valuable flow for enterprise architectures: exchange the inbound user token for a scoped downstream token with no additional consent flow. The agent's identity and the caller's identity are both preserved for zero-trust authorization downstream, and developers never handle the inbound token or the client secret.

Grant typeStandardHow the inbound JWT is used
TOKEN_EXCHANGERFC 8693Sent as subject_token (urn:ietf:params:oauth:token-type:jwt)
JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANTRFC 7523 §2.1Sent as assertion; no actor token required

actorTokenContent (TOKEN_EXCHANGE only)

ValueWhat AgentCore sends as the actor token
M2MPerforms client-credentials authentication and sends the resulting access token
AWS_IAM_ID_TOKEN_JWTCalls sts:GetWebIdentityToken and sends the resulting JWT (requires outbound web identity federation enabled)
NONENo actor token — the IdP derives actor identity from client authentication alone
json
{
  "name": "sample-obo-custom",
  "credentialProviderVendor": "CustomOauth2",
  "oauth2ProviderConfigInput": {
    "customOauth2ProviderConfig": {
      "oauthDiscovery": { "discoveryUrl": "https://my.idp.example.com/.well-known/openid-configuration" },
      "clientId": "your-client-id",
      "clientSecret": "your-client-secret",
      "clientAuthenticationMethod": "CLIENT_SECRET_BASIC",
      "onBehalfOfTokenExchangeConfig": {
        "grantType": "TOKEN_EXCHANGE",
        "tokenExchangeGrantTypeConfig": {
          "actorTokenContent": "M2M",
          "actorTokenScopes": ["scope1", "scope2"]
        }
      }
    }
  }
}
bash
# 1. exchange the inbound JWT for a workload access token
aws bedrock-agentcore get-workload-access-token-for-jwt \
  --workload-name sample-workload --user-token "INBOUND_JWT"

# 2. exchange that for a downstream token — no consent flow
aws bedrock-agentcore get-resource-oauth2-token \
  --resource-credential-provider-name sample-obo-provider \
  --oauth2-flow ON_BEHALF_OF_TOKEN_EXCHANGE \
  --scopes "sample-scope" \
  --workload-identity-token "WORKLOAD_ACCESS_TOKEN"
Microsoft is pre-configured

The MicrosoftOauth2 vendor defaults to JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANT and automatically adds requested_token_use=on_behalf_of unless you override it — so Entra ID OBO works with almost no configuration.

17. SDK decorators #

The bedrock-agentcore Python SDK reduces credential retrieval to a decorator that injects the credential as a keyword argument.

DecoratorInjectsKey parameters
@requires_access_tokenaccess_token: strprovider_name, scopes, auth_flow, on_auth_url, force_authentication, callback_url
@requires_api_keyapi_key: strprovider_name
python
from bedrock_agentcore.identity.auth import requires_access_token, requires_api_key

@requires_access_token(provider_name="google-provider",
                       scopes=["https://www.googleapis.com/auth/drive"],
                       auth_flow="USER_FEDERATION",
                       on_auth_url=lambda u: print("Authorize:", u))
async def user_delegated(*, access_token: str): ...

@requires_access_token(provider_name="system-provider",
                       scopes=["service-account-scope"],
                       auth_flow="M2M")
async def machine_only(*, access_token: str): ...

@requires_api_key(provider_name="stripe-provider")
async def with_api_key(*, api_key: str): ...
Decorators are async and keyword-only

The credential arrives as a keyword-only argument (*, access_token) on an async def function. Wrapping synchronous agent code means an asyncio.run() boundary — plan for it rather than discovering it mid-integration. Also remember that under USER_FEDERATION the decorated call may block on a human, so it should never sit on a latency-critical path.

18. Private IdPs & VPC #

Since April 2026, Identity can reach OAuth 2.0 identity providers hosted inside your VPC without exposing them to the internet, using Amazon VPC Lattice resource gateways. This works for both inbound JWT authorization and outbound credential providers.

Managed VPC resourceSelf-managed Lattice
ComplexityLower — AgentCore manages the resource lifecycleHigher — you own the Lattice resource configuration
Cross-accountnoyes
Control & visibilityLimitedFull
Best forSingle-account, data-residency or compliance needsEnterprise, multi-account, strict governance
json
// Managed
"privateEndpoint": {
  "managedVpcResource": {
    "vpcIdentifier": "vpc-0abc123def456",
    "subnetIds": ["subnet-0abc123", "subnet-0def456"],
    "securityGroupIds": ["sg-0abc123def"],
    "endpointIpAddressType": "IPV4"
  }
}

// Self-managed
"privateEndpoint": {
  "selfManagedLatticeResource": {
    "resourceConfigurationIdentifier": "arn:aws:vpc-lattice:us-east-1:123456789012:resourceconfiguration/rcfg-abc123"
  }
}

Prerequisites: the IdP's discovery, JWKS and token endpoints reachable from the given subnets; security groups permitting inbound HTTPS on 443; iam:CreateServiceLinkedRole and ec2:CreateNetworkInterface for managed Lattice.

TLS is the usual blocker

Discovery URLs must be HTTPS with a publicly trusted certificate. Internal IdPs fronted by a private CA will fail. The standard workaround is an ALB with a public ACM certificate in front of the private IdP. privateEndpointOverrides lets you map individual domains to different endpoints when the IdP spans hosts.

19. IAM & scope-down #

How the two gates compose

The agent's IAM role decides which credential providers it may call. The workload access token decides whose credentials come back. A successful API call does not imply a credential — if no token is stored for that user, you get an authorization URL instead.

19.1 Allow one OAuth provider

json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "GetResourceOauth2Token",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:GetResourceOauth2Token"],
    "Resource": [
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default",
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default/workload-identity/WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_NAME",
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:token-vault/default",
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:token-vault/default/oauth2-credential-provider/PROVIDER_NAME"
    ]
  }]
}

19.2 Allow one API key provider

json
{
  "Sid": "GetResourceApiKey",
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:GetResourceApiKey"],
  "Resource": [
    "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default",
    "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default/workload-identity/WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_NAME",
    "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:token-vault/default",
    "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:token-vault/default/api-key/PROVIDER_NAME"
  ]
}

19.3 Guardrail denies

json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    { "Sid": "DenySensitiveProviders", "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": ["bedrock-agentcore:GetResourceApiKey", "bedrock-agentcore:GetResourceOauth2Token"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:token-vault/default/api-key/finance-provider",
        "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:123456789012:token-vault/default/oauth2-credential-provider/hr-provider"
      ] },
    { "Sid": "DenyUnverifiedUserIdentity", "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": "bedrock-agentcore:GetWorkloadAccessTokenForUserId",
      "Resource": "*" }
  ]
}

19.4 Service-linked role

Since October 2025 new agents use a service-linked role for identity permissions. The control-plane role needs permission to create it:

json
{
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": "iam:CreateServiceLinkedRole",
  "Resource": "arn:aws:iam::*:role/aws-service-role/runtime-identity.bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com/AWSServiceRoleForBedrockAgentCoreRuntimeIdentity",
  "Condition": {
    "StringEquals": { "iam:AWSServiceName": "runtime-identity.bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com" }
  }
}

Additional controls: resource-based policies (Mar 2026) let a credential provider grant access without editing every caller's role, and the IAM condition keys bedrock-agentcore:Subnets / :SecurityGroups (Jan 2026) enforce approved network configurations.

19.5 Practices that hold up

  • Name every provider ARN explicitly in Resource; never wildcard the token vault.
  • One workload identity and one role per agent or trust tier — scoping is only as fine as your identities.
  • Use explicit Deny for sensitive providers; denies always win.
  • Audit with CloudTrail and re-review the policy set as providers are added.
  • Validate in development first — a scope-down mistake fails closed and looks like an outage.

20. Observability #

NamespaceMetricMeasures
AWS/UsageCallCountTotal Identity API calls
ThrottleCountThrottled calls
AWS/Bedrock-AgentCoreWorkloadAccessTokenFetchSuccess / Failures / ThrottlesWorkload access token operations, failures broken out by exception type
ResourceAccessTokenFetchSuccess / Failures / ThrottlesOAuth2 resource token operations
ApiKeyFetchSuccess / Failures / ThrottlesAPI key retrievals

Dimensions: WorkloadIdentity, WorkloadIdentityDirectory, TokenVault, ProviderName, FlowType (USER_FEDERATION / M2M), ExceptionType.

Span data lands in the aws/spans log group with timestamp, resource_arn, account_id, request_id, trace_id, span_id, service_name, operation and request/response payload fields. CloudWatch Logs, S3 and Firehose are all supported destinations.

The dimension that earns its keep

FlowType split by ProviderName tells you how much of your traffic is paying the consent tax. A rising USER_FEDERATION failure rate on one provider almost always means expired refresh tokens or a revoked OAuth app — user-visible breakage that is otherwise silent until someone complains.

21. Quotas & limits #

21.1 Resources

QuotaDefaultAdjustable
Workload identities11,000yes
OAuth2 credential providers50yes
API key credential providers50yes
Payment credential providers50yes

21.2 API rates (per account, per region)

API groupRateAdjustable
GetWorkloadAccessToken / ...ForJWT / ...ForUserId200 TPS eachyes
GetResourceOauth2Token / GetResourceApiKey / GetResourcePaymentToken200 TPS eachyes
CompleteResourceTokenAuth100 TPSyes
Workload identity management (Create/Get/Update/Delete/List)20 TPS eachyes
Credential provider management — OAuth2, API key, payment (Create/Get/Update/Delete/List)20 TPS eachyes
The quota to watch

Not the TPS — 200 TPS of token retrieval is generous. It is 50 credential providers per type. An enterprise with a dozen SaaS integrations across dev, staging and production hits that quickly. Decide early whether providers are shared across environments or duplicated per environment, and raise the quota before it blocks a launch.

22. Pricing #

Verify before you budget

Rates below are published US on-demand rates as of 18 Aug 2026 and vary by region.

UsageRate
Via AgentCore Runtime or AgentCore GatewayNo additional charge
Standalone (self-hosted agents calling the APIs directly)$0.010 per 1,000 tokens or API keys requested

At standalone rates, ten million credential retrievals a month costs $100. In practice this line item is almost never material — which is worth stating plainly, because "we'll just use environment variables" is a decision that should be made on security grounds, not cost.

Adjacent costs to remember: KMS operations for private key JWT signing, Secrets Manager charges for EXTERNAL secrets, and VPC Lattice charges for private IdP connectivity.

23. Security best practices #

Deny ForUserId in production

It accepts an unverified string. Grant ForJWT and add an explicit Deny — remembering that BedrockAgentCoreFullAccess includes the unsafe variant.

Namespace user identifiers by IdP

cognito+user123, auth0+user123. Prevents cross-directory collisions in the vault that are both a leak and an unrecoverable migration.

One workload identity per trust tier

Scope-down policies are only as fine-grained as your identities. A shared identity makes every policy a formality.

Prefer private key JWT for high-value providers

A KMS-held signing key cannot be exfiltrated and every use is auditable, unlike a bearer client secret.

Reference your own Secrets Manager secrets

EXTERNAL gives you the CMK, rotation schedule, tagging and resource policy you already have governance for.

Validate the live session at the 3LO callback

Resolve the user from cookies or session storage — never from a cache keyed by session_id, which defeats session binding.

Always send customState

An opaque per-request value, verified on return, is your CSRF defence for the consent flow.

Isolate payment credentials

Own identity, own role, explicit allow-list, Cedar limits on amounts, human approval above a threshold.

Request only the scopes you need

Scopes are requested per call. A read-only task should not ask for write scopes — users see the consent screen and notice.

Pin the caller with allowedWorkloadConfiguration

Restrict which hosting environments may invoke a runtime, so nobody bypasses the gateway and its policy engine.

24. Troubleshooting #

SymptomLikely causeFix
“You must register and use at least one ResourceOauth2ReturnUrl”Return URL not registered on the workload identityupdate-workload-identity --allowed-resource-oauth2-return-urls https://…
Redirect-mismatch error at the OAuth providerThe provider's callbackUrl wasn't added to the OAuth appRe-read callbackUrl from GetOauth2CredentialProvider and register it
401 with WWW-AuthenticateMissing or expired bearer tokenRefresh; verify iss, aud, client_id and exp against the authorizer config
403 AccessDeniedException fetching a credentialRole lacks GetResourceOauth2Token/GetResourceApiKey on that provider ARNAdd all four ARNs — directory, workload identity, vault, provider (§19.1)
Call succeeds but returns an authorization URL instead of a tokenNo cached credential for this user under this workloadExpected on first use — run the consent flow; consider warming consent at onboarding
Authorization URL no longer works10-minute expiry, or it was opened by a different userRegenerate; present in-session rather than emailing or queueing
Consent completes but the token never appearsCompleteResourceTokenAuth not called, or called with the wrong user identifierVerify the callback resolves the user from the live session and passes the session URI
Wrong user's data returnedUser IDs collide across IdPsNamespace identifiers (cognito+, auth0+); audit existing vault entries
Agent on Runtime can't fetch a workload access tokenRuntime-managed identities cannot retrieve them directlyRead the injected WorkloadAccessToken from the invocation context
Private IdP discovery failsPrivate CA certificate, or endpoints unreachable from the subnetsFront the IdP with an ALB using a public ACM certificate; check security groups on 443
ServiceQuotaExceededException creating a provider50 providers of that type already existConsolidate, or request a Service Quotas increase
Service-linked role creation failsControl-plane role lacks iam:CreateServiceLinkedRoleAdd the statement in §19.4
OBO exchange rejected by the IdPWrong grant type, or the IdP doesn't support RFC 8693 / RFC 7523Try JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANT; check whether an actor token is required
Decorator raises about a missing keyword argumentCredential is injected as keyword-only on an async functionUse async def fn(*, access_token: str) and asyncio.run()

24.1 Debug checklist

bash
# 1. Does the workload identity exist, and are return URLs registered?
aws bedrock-agentcore-control list-workload-identities
aws bedrock-agentcore-control get-workload-identity --name MY_AGENT_IDENTITY

# 2. Is the provider READY, and what is its callback URL?
aws bedrock-agentcore-control get-oauth2-credential-provider --name google-provider

# 3. Can I get a workload access token at all?
aws bedrock-agentcore get-workload-access-token-for-jwt \
  --workload-name MY_AGENT_IDENTITY --user-token "$INBOUND_JWT"

# 4. Does the resource token come back, or an authorization URL?
aws bedrock-agentcore get-resource-oauth2-token \
  --resource-credential-provider-name google-provider \
  --oauth2-flow USER_FEDERATION --scopes "https://www.googleapis.com/auth/drive.readonly" \
  --workload-identity-token "$WORKLOAD_ACCESS_TOKEN"

# 5. What do the metrics say?
#    CloudWatch → AWS/Bedrock-AgentCore → ResourceAccessTokenFetchFailures
#    split by ProviderName, FlowType and ExceptionType

Appendix A — Release timeline #

Jul 2026
Private key JWT client authentication
Authenticate to IdPs with KMS-signed JWTs instead of shared client secrets. RS256/PS256/ES256; keys never leave KMS; all signing in CloudTrail. Works across M2M, OBO and user-delegated flows.
Jun 2026
Reference existing Secrets Manager secrets
EXTERNAL secret sources let you own creation, classification, CMKs, tagging, rotation and resource policies.
Apr 2026
VPC egress · on-behalf-of exchange · new regions
Private IdPs via managed or self-managed VPC Lattice with private DNS; OBO token exchange removing repeat consent; GA in São Paulo and Canada Central.
Mar 2026
Resource-based policies · control-plane PrivateLink
Granular access without editing caller roles; PrivateLink for Identity control-plane operations.
Jan 2026
VPC condition keys
bedrock-agentcore:Subnets and :SecurityGroups for enforcing approved network configurations.
Oct 2025
GA · runtime identity service-linked role
Identity generally available with AgentCore. AWSServiceRoleForBedrockAgentCoreRuntimeIdentity manages workload access tokens and OAuth credentials.
Jul 2025
Preview launch
Identity ships as part of the initial AgentCore preview.

Appendix C — Glossary #

TermMeaning
Workload identityAn agent's durable identity in AWS; the subject of credential decisions.
Workload access tokenToken carrying agent identity + end-user identity; unlocks the vault.
Token vaultManaged encrypted store for client credentials, API keys and cached user tokens.
Credential providerConfiguration for obtaining a credential for one downstream resource.
Resource tokenThe short-lived downstream credential returned to the agent.
2LO / M2MTwo-legged OAuth — agent uses its own client credentials, no user.
3LO / USER_FEDERATIONThree-legged OAuth — user consents; token scoped to that user.
OBOOn-behalf-of token exchange (RFC 8693 / RFC 7523) — downstream token without new consent.
Actor tokenIn RFC 8693, the token representing the agent acting on the subject's behalf.
Session bindingVerifying that the initiator of an authorization request is the grantor of consent.
Session URIurn:ietf:params:oauth:request_uri:… identifying an in-flight consent session.
Private key JWTClient authentication by KMS-signed JWT instead of a shared secret.
MANAGED / EXTERNALSecret held by AgentCore, vs. referenced from your own Secrets Manager secret.

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity — Technical Reference · revision 18 August 2026. Unofficial documentation compiled from public AWS sources; not affiliated with or endorsed by Amazon Web Services. Verify quotas, pricing and API shapes against current AWS documentation before production use.

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability

A complete technical reference for tracing, debugging and monitoring AI agents on AWS — the session/trace/span model, service-emitted metrics, Transaction Search, ADOT instrumentation, log delivery, the CloudWatch GenAI Observability console, cross-account setup, Evaluations, cost control and troubleshooting.

Document revision: 18 August 2026  ·  Service status: Generally available (GA 13 Oct 2025)  ·  Metric namespace: AWS/Bedrock-AgentCore  ·  Console: CloudWatch → GenAI Observability
Fast-moving service

Observability has changed materially since GA — unified span destination became the default for new agents on 20 July 2026, ActiveSessionCount and PrivateLink for Evaluations arrived in July 2026, one-click enablement for Memory and Gateway in March 2026, cross-account monitoring and sub-10-second trace latency in April 2026, and Evaluations went GA in March 2026. Figures below reflect AWS documentation as of the revision date.

Architect's brief — read this first #

The parts that shape architecture rather than the parts you can look up. Everything here is expanded in the reference sections that follow.

0.1 The one idea everything else follows from

Observability is not a service you provision — it is a contract with two halves, and only one half is automatic.

Metrics (free, automatic)Traces & spans (opt-in, instrumented)
What you getCounters and gauges: invocations, errors, throttles, latency, session counts, CPU/memory consumedCausality: which tool was called, with what arguments, how long the model took, where the reasoning went wrong
Setup requiredNoneEnable CloudWatch Transaction Search once per account, and run under ADOT instrumentation
Answers“Is it broken, and how badly?”“Why is it broken?”
The failure mode this creates

Teams deploy, see metrics populate in CloudWatch, conclude observability is working — and then discover during their first production incident that they have counters but no causality. Both switches are easy to miss and neither errors if absent. Enable Transaction Search and confirm spans are arriving before you need them, not during an outage. → §7

0.2 The decisions you cannot walk back

Session ID discipline is the whole product

Sessions, traces and spans form a hierarchy that only exists if you pass a consistent runtimeSessionId across a conversation. Get it wrong and every turn becomes an orphan session: the GenAI console's sessions view is meaningless, cross-turn debugging is impossible, and no retrofit recovers historical data. → §12

Sampling percentage is a permanent trade

Transaction Search samples — 1% is free, higher costs money. Sample too low and the trace you need during an incident was never captured. Sampling decisions are not retroactive: unsampled traffic is gone. → §18

Payloads land in logs by default

request_payload and response_payload are logged fields, and spans capture tool arguments. That is exactly what makes debugging possible — and exactly how PII ends up in CloudWatch with default retention. Decide redaction policy before go-live. → §20

One default that changed under people

Spans used to go to the shared aws/spans log group. Since 20 July 2026, newly created agents deliver spans to their own log group instead (/aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/AGENT_ID-ENDPOINT). Older agents stay on the old behaviour until you set UNIFIED_TRACES_DESTINATION_ENABLED=true. If half your fleet's traces appear somewhere your queries don't look, this is why. → §10

0.3 What will surprise you in production

SurpriseWhy it happensWhat to do
Observability has no AgentCore charge — but a real CloudWatch bill AWS charges nothing for the feature; you pay standard CloudWatch rates for ingestion, storage and queries of every span, log and metric. Agents are verbose: one turn can emit dozens of spans with full payloads. Model the ingestion volume before enabling 100% sampling fleet-wide. → §18
Traces are not real-time Put-to-get latency for a complete trace is under ~10 seconds (improved April 2026 from 10s spans / 30s logs). Fine for debugging, wrong for a synchronous control loop. Don't build runtime logic that reads back its own traces.
Spans and logs need explicit enablement for several services Metrics are default everywhere; spans and logs are opt-in for Agent, Memory, Tools and Policy resources. One-click enablement covers Runtime, Browser, Code Interpreter, and — since March 2026 — Memory and Gateway. Verify per resource type. → §5
DISABLE_ADOT_OBSERVABILITY is all-or-nothing Setting it unsets every AgentCore default ADOT variable so you can ship to a third-party backend. You lose the GenAI Observability console entirely — including the views Evaluations depends on. Choose one primary backend deliberately. → §15
Evaluations cost real money per token Built-in evaluators bill $0.0024 per 1K input and $0.012 per 1K output tokens; custom evaluators $1.50 per 1,000 evaluations plus model usage. Online evaluation samples a configurable percentage of live traffic — set it deliberately, not at 100%. → §17
The console is only as good as your span names Trajectory views, span trees and Evaluations all read OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. Use a supported instrumentation library (OpenInference, OpenLLMetry, OpenLIT, Traceloop) rather than hand-rolling span names. → §8

0.4 The thing that actually determines value

Agent observability differs from service observability in one way that matters: the interesting failures are semantic, not operational. A 200 OK response containing a confidently wrong answer is invisible to every metric on the dashboard. Latency is fine, error rate is zero, and the user got garbage.

That is why the stack has three layers, and why stopping at the first two is the common mistake:

1 · Metrics — is it up?

Invocations, errors, throttles, latency, active sessions. Free, immediate, and blind to correctness.

2 · Traces — what did it do?

The reasoning trajectory: tool calls, arguments, model turns, retries. Needs Transaction Search plus ADOT.

3 · Evaluations — was it any good?

LLM-as-judge scoring over those traces: goal success, correctness, tool-selection accuracy, harmfulness. The only layer that sees semantic failure.

Layer 3 is built directly on layer 2 — Evaluations reads OTEL traces with GenAI semantic conventions. So instrumentation quality is not just a debugging convenience; it is the precondition for ever measuring whether your agent is correct.

0.5 The pipeline in one page

  agent (Runtime / Gateway / Memory / self-hosted)
        │
        ├── service-emitted METRICS ─────────────────► AWS/Bedrock-AgentCore namespace   [automatic]
        │                                              invocations · errors · latency
        │                                              sessions · CPU-hours · GB-hours
        │
        ├── APPLICATION_LOGS ───────────────────────► /aws/bedrock-agentcore/runtimes/…  [automatic]
        │                                              incl. request_payload / response_payload
        │
        └── ADOT / OpenTelemetry  ──────────────────► spans                              [opt-in ×2]
                 opentelemetry-instrument                 │
                 + Transaction Search enabled             │
                                                          ▼
                                     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
                                     │  CloudWatch GenAI Observability      │
                                     │  agents view · sessions · traces     │
                                     │  span tree · trajectory diagram      │
                                     └───────────────┬──────────────────────┘
                                                     │
                                                     ▼
                                        AgentCore Evaluations
                                        online · on-demand · batch
                                        14 built-in evaluators

0.6 When you don't need to do anything

You already have an OTEL backend

Set DISABLE_ADOT_OBSERVABILITY=true and point OTLP at Langfuse, Datadog or Dynatrace. You give up the GenAI console and Evaluations.

Pre-production experiments

Default metrics plus agentcore logs is genuinely enough while you're still changing the agent daily.

Agents outside AWS

ADOT can ship telemetry from on-premises, GCP or Azure into the same dashboards via SigV4 — no migration required. → §14

1. Overview #

Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability provides tracing, debugging and monitoring for AI agents in production. It emits telemetry in standard OpenTelemetry format, stores everything in Amazon CloudWatch, and surfaces purpose-built views — sessions, traces, span trees, trajectory diagrams — through the CloudWatch GenAI Observability console.

3
Telemetry tiers
~10 s
Put-to-get trace latency
1%
Free sampling tier
14
Built-in evaluators
$0
AgentCore feature charge
100k
Cross-account log groups

1.1 What it gives you

Default metrics with no work

Agents, Gateway, Memory, Identity, Policy, Payments and built-in tools all publish metrics automatically to AWS/Bedrock-AgentCore.

GenAI-aware trace views

Span trees that bundle repeated spans, visual span icons, default filters that hide infrastructure noise, and trajectory diagrams aligned with industry conventions.

OTEL-native

Standard OpenTelemetry output means existing tooling works, and third-party backends are a configuration change rather than a rewrite.

Anywhere the agent runs

ADOT ships telemetry from AgentCore Runtime, Lambda, ECS, on-premises or another cloud into the same dashboards.

Cross-account rollup

CloudWatch OAM links let one monitoring account see agents, sessions and traces across an entire organisation.

Evaluations on top

LLM-as-judge scoring over the same traces, online or in batch, with ground-truth support and CI/CD regression testing.

2. Where Observability fits in AgentCore #

ServiceWhat it emitsNotes
RuntimeInvocations, errors, latency, session counts, ActiveSessionCount, CPU/memory hours, WebSocket metrics, spans, application and usage logsThe richest emitter; spans require ADOT
GatewayInvocations, throttles, errors, latency, duration, TargetExecutionTime, target type, spansOne-click enablement since March 2026

3. Free vs. instrumented #

TelemetryRequiresAvailable for
MetricsNothingEvery AgentCore resource type
Application logsNothing for Runtime; delivery configuration for Memory/GatewayRuntime by default; others via one-click or delivery APIs
Sessions viewConsistent runtimeSessionId on every invocationRuntime
Traces & spansTransaction Search enabled + ADOT instrumentationRuntime, Gateway, Memory, Tools, Policy
Custom spans and metricsExplicit instrumentation in agent codeAnywhere
EvaluationsTraces with GenAI semantic conventionsRuntime endpoints or CloudWatch log groups
The minimum viable setup

Enable Transaction Search once per account, deploy agents on Runtime with the AgentCore CLI (which wires ADOT automatically), and pass a stable session ID per conversation. That covers metrics, logs, traces, sessions and the console — and takes about ten minutes. Everything after that is refinement.

4. Sessions, traces, spans #

  SESSION — the whole conversation between a user and an agent
  │  unique id · context persistence · state · isolation
  │
  ├── TRACE — one request/response cycle (may fan out to other agents)
  │   │  timestamps · inputs · processing steps · errors · response
  │   │
  │   ├── SPAN "parse input"
  │   ├── SPAN "retrieve context"
  │   │     └── SPAN "memory.retrieve_records"
  │   ├── SPAN "invoke model"
  │   │     └── SPAN "tool: get_weather"
  │   └── SPAN "format output"
  │
  └── TRACE — the next turn in the same conversation
      └── …
LevelRepresentsAnswersWhere it lives
SessionA complete interaction context, initialization to terminationEngagement patterns; agent performance over a conversationCloudWatch metrics + GenAI Observability sessions view
TraceOne request/response cycleWhat the agent did for this request, and where time wentCloudWatch Logs + Transaction Search (needs ADOT)
SpanA discrete unit of work with parent/child nestingWhich exact operation failed or was slowspans log stream (needs ADOT)

Span attributes carry operation name, start/end timestamps, parent-child relationships, tags, events, status and resource metrics. AgentCore's own InvokeAgentRuntime spans add aws.operation.name, aws.resource.arn, aws.request_id, aws.agent.id, aws.endpoint.name, aws.account.id, session.id, latency_ms, error_type, aws.resource.type, aws.xray.origin and aws.region.

How to actually use the hierarchy

Debug top-down. A user complains → find the session → spot the anomalous trace → drill to the span where the tool returned nonsense or the model looped. Starting from raw logs on a busy agent is hopeless; starting from the session is tractable. This is the entire argument for session ID discipline.

6. Runtime metrics reference #

Namespace AWS/Bedrock-AgentCore, batched at one-minute intervals. Dimensions: Service (AgentCore.Runtime, AgentCore.Browser, AgentCore.CodeInterpreter), Resource (agent ARN), Name (AgentName::EndpointName).

6.1 Core

MetricMeasuresUnit

SystemErrorsServer-side failuresCount
UserErrorsClient-side failuresCount
TotalErrorsSystem + user errorsCount / %
LatencyEnd-to-end request to final responseMilliseconds

ActiveSessionCountCurrently active sessions (real-time gauge, published once a minute)Count

6.2 Streaming and resource usage

CPUUsed-vCPUHoursvCPU consumed — the CPU half of your Runtime bill
MemoryUsed-GBHoursMemory consumed — the memory half of your Runtime bill

6.3 Error type codes

Classerror_typeHTTP
UserInvocationError.Validation400

InvocationError.Conflict409

ThrottlingInvocationError.Throttling429

Two metrics that pay for themselves

ActiveSessionCount against your session quota is the earliest warning of leaked sessions — the most common AgentCore production surprise. And CPUUsed-vCPUHours plus MemoryUsed-GBHours are your Runtime bill, so graphing them turns cost from a month-end discovery into a live signal.

6.4 Log record fields

Log typeFields
APPLICATION_LOGStimestamp, resource_arn, event_timestamp, account_id, request_id, session_id, trace_id, span_id, service_name, operation, request_payload, response_payload
USAGE_LOGS (1-second granularity)event_timestamp, resource_arn, service.name, cloud.provider, cloud.region, account.id, resource.id, session.id, agent.name, elapsed_time_seconds, agent.runtime.vcpu.hours.used, agent.runtime.memory.gb_hours.used

7. Enabling Transaction Search #

A one-time, per-account, per-region prerequisite for any trace or span data. Without it, ADOT will happily emit spans that nothing indexes.

7.1 Console

  1. Open the CloudWatch console → Application Signals (APM) → Transaction search.
  2. Choose Enable Transaction Search.
  3. Tick ingest spans as structured logs.
  4. Set the sampling percentage — 1% is free.
  5. Save, then allow ~10 minutes before spans become searchable.

7.2 CLI

bash
aws logs put-resource-policy --policy-name MyResourcePolicy --policy-document '{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "TransactionSearchXRayAccess",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "xray.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": "logs:PutLogEvents",
    "Resource": [
      "arn:aws:logs:us-west-2:123456789012:log-group:aws/spans:*",
      "arn:aws:logs:us-west-2:123456789012:log-group:/aws/application-signals/data:*"
    ],
    "Condition": {
      "ArnLike":      { "aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:xray:us-west-2:123456789012:*" },
      "StringEquals": { "aws:SourceAccount": "123456789012" }
    }
  }]
}'

aws xray update-trace-segment-destination --destination CloudWatchLogs

# Sampling — 1% is the free tier; raise deliberately
aws xray update-indexing-rule --name "Default" \
  --rule '{"Probabilistic": {"DesiredSamplingPercentage": 5}}'
Sampling is a real trade, not a formality

At 1% you will frequently find that the exact trace a user is complaining about was never captured. At 100% you pay CloudWatch ingestion on every span of every turn. A common compromise is high sampling in pre-production and a modest percentage in production, raised temporarily during incident investigation — remembering that raising it is not retroactive.

8. Instrumenting with ADOT #

bash
# requirements.txt
aws-opentelemetry-distro>=0.18.0
boto3

# run under auto-instrumentation
opentelemetry-instrument python my_agent.py
dockerfile
CMD ["opentelemetry-instrument", "python", "main.py"]

For direct code deployment, the entry point carries it:

python
"entryPoint": ["opentelemetry-instrument", "main.py"]

8.1 Framework instrumentation libraries

Auto-instrumentation covers AWS SDK calls; agent-framework semantics come from a GenAI instrumentation library. AgentCore supports:

OpenInferenceOpenLLMetryOpenLITTraceloop

These work with Strands, LangChain/LangGraph and CrewAI, and are what produce spans the GenAI console and Evaluations can interpret.

Hand-rolled span names are a dead end

The trajectory view, the span-tree bundling and every built-in evaluator read OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. Spans named step_1, step_2 will render as an uninformative tree and score badly under evaluators that expect to identify tool calls and model turns. Use a supported library; add custom spans around it, not instead of it.

8.2 Version requirements

aws-opentelemetry-distro >= 0.18.0 is required for the unified span destination. Older versions still work but only against the shared aws/spans log group.

9. Environment variables #

9.1 AgentCore-hosted agents

The platform supplies the rest; these four are the contract.

bash
OTEL_PYTHON_DISTRO=aws_distro
